1. **Declarative GitOps Workflow:** Maintaining Kubernetes manifests and Helm charts in a Git repository (Single Source of Truth), which Argo CD monitors and synchronizes to the target cluster.
2. **Automated & Manual Sync:** Configuring Application CRDs with automated sync policies, self-healing (`selfHeal: true`), and automated pruning (`prune: true`) to prevent manual drift in the cluster.

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Explain what Helm charts are.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **Helm Chart** is a packaged collection of pre-configured Kubernetes YAML templates and configuration files. It acts as the package manager for Kubernetes (similar to `apt` or `yum` for Linux, or `npm` for Node.js).

### Key Components of a Helm Chart:
```text
my-app/
├── Chart.yaml          # Metadata about the chart (name, version, appVersion)
├── values.yaml         # Default configuration values for templates
├── charts/             # Dependency charts (subcharts)
└── templates/          # Kubernetes resource manifest templates
    ├── deployment.yaml
    ├── service.yaml
    ├── ingress.yaml
    ├── hpa.yaml
    └── _helpers.tpl    # Reusable template helper definitions
```

### Why We Use Helm Charts:
1. **Parameterization:** Replaces hardcoded values in YAML with Go template variables (e.g., `{{ .Values.replicaCount }}`).
2. **Reusability Across Environments:** The same manifest templates can be deployed to Dev, Staging, or Prod simply by passing different `values.yaml` files.
3. **Release Management & Rollback:** Helm tracks release revisions, making it easy to upgrade or rollback with a single command (`helm rollback`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you explain how Helm charts work with a concrete example, such as passing values to a chart during installation?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Helm works by taking a parameterized **template file** and injecting values from a **values file** or CLI arguments to generate standard Kubernetes manifests.

### Concrete Example:

**1. Template (`templates/deployment.yaml`):**
```yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-app
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: web
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
          ports:
            - containerPort: {{ .Values.service.port }}
```

**2. Default Values (`values.yaml`):**
```yaml
replicaCount: 1
image:
  repository: myrepo/app
  tag: "1.0.0"
service:
  port: 8080
```

**3. Environment Override (`values-prod.yaml`):**
```yaml
replicaCount: 5
image:
  tag: "1.2.0"
```

**4. Installation Methods:**
- **Method 1: Passing a Values File:**
  ```bash
  helm install prod-release ./my-app -f values-prod.yaml -n production
  ```
- **Method 2: Overriding individual parameters via `--set`:**
  ```bash
  helm install prod-release ./my-app --set replicaCount=3 --set image.tag="v2.1"
  ```
During execution, Helm merges the default `values.yaml` with the override flags/files, renders the manifest with 5 replicas and tag `1.2.0`, and applies it to the cluster.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you prefer working with native self-managed Kubernetes or with the AWS managed Kubernetes service (EKS)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I strongly prefer working with **AWS managed Kubernetes service (Amazon EKS)** for production environments.

### Reasons:
1. **Reduced Operational Overhead:** In self-managed Kubernetes (e.g., using `kubeadm`), the team is responsible for master node high availability, etcd clustering, manual backup/restore, OS patching, and control plane upgrades. In EKS, AWS manages the control plane and etcd with a 99.95% SLA.
2. **Deep AWS Integration:** EKS integrates natively with IAM (IRSA - IAM Roles for Service Accounts), AWS VPC CNI (giving pods real VPC IP addresses), Application Load Balancers via AWS Load Balancer Controller, and AWS KMS for secret encryption.
3. **Automated Worker Node Upgrades:** Managed node groups and Karpenter allow effortless, automated node provisioning and zero-downtime rolling upgrades.

*Note: Self-managed Kubernetes is great for learning internals or specialized on-premise/bare-metal setups, but EKS is far more reliable and cost-effective for enterprise cloud environments.*
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Do you know about the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) in Kubernetes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, the **Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA)** is a Kubernetes component that automatically scales the number of pod replicas in a Deployment, ReplicaSet, or StatefulSet up or down based on observed resource utilization (such as CPU or memory) or custom application metrics.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Explain what the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) is and how it works in Kubernetes.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### How HPA Works:
HPA operates as a continuous control loop managed by the Kubernetes Controller Manager:

1. **Metric Collection:** Every period (default 15 seconds), HPA queries the **Metrics Server** (or Prometheus Adapter for custom metrics) to get current utilization metrics for the pods.
2. **Target Calculation:** It compares current metric values against the target threshold defined in the HPA manifest using the formula:
   $$\text{Desired Replicas} = \left\lceil \text{Current Replicas} \times \left( \frac{\text{Current Metric Value}}{\text{Target Metric Value}} \right) \right\rceil$$
3. **Scaling Action:** If current utilization exceeds the target, HPA increases the replica count up to the configured `maxReplicas`. When utilization drops, it gradually scales down to `minReplicas` (with a default stabilization window to avoid flapping).

### Prerequisite:
Pods **must have `resources.requests` defined** in their container specification. Without resource requests, HPA cannot calculate the utilization percentage and will report `<unknown>`.

### Example HPA Manifest:
```yaml
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: web-hpa
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: web-app
  minReplicas: 2
  maxReplicas: 10
  metrics:
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: cpu
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: 70
```
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is AWS DynamoDB, and when should and should you not use it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Amazon DynamoDB** is a fully managed, serverless, NoSQL key-value and document database service provided by AWS that delivers single-digit millisecond performance at any scale.

### When to Use DynamoDB:
- **Known Access Patterns:** Workloads where query patterns are key-value based (querying by partition key and sort key).
- **High Throughput & Low Latency:** Applications requiring sub-10ms response times at high scale (e.g., e-commerce shopping carts, user session stores, real-time gaming leaderboards, IoT telemetry).
- **Serverless Architectures:** Event-driven apps using AWS Lambda where you want zero server management, automatic scaling, and pay-per-request pricing.

### When NOT to Use DynamoDB:
- **Complex Relational Queries:** When applications require table joins, complex multi-table aggregations, or arbitrary SQL queries (use Amazon RDS or Aurora instead).
- **Ad-hoc / Unknown Query Patterns:** When query requirements change frequently without predefined indexes.
- **Large Blob Storage:** Storing large files or media objects over 400 KB (the maximum DynamoDB item size is 400 KB; use Amazon S3 instead).
- **OLAP / Heavy Analytics:** Data warehousing or analytical reporting (use Amazon Redshift or Snowflake instead).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Setting aside Terraform state locking, in what use cases should you use DynamoDB and in what use cases should you not use it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Practical Production Use Cases:

**1. Good Use Cases for DynamoDB:**
- **User Session & Authentication Token Store:** Storing user logins, JWT session IDs, or refresh tokens where lookup is strictly by `UserId` or `TokenId`.
- **E-Commerce Shopping Cart:** Fast updates, highly concurrent writes, and automatic item cleanup using **DynamoDB TTL (Time to Live)** to expire abandoned carts.
- **IoT Event & Device Metadata Storage:** Ingesting millions of sensor state records partitioned by `DeviceId` and sorted by `Timestamp`.
- **Feature Flags & Application Configs:** Fast key-value lookups with in-memory caching via DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX).

**2. Scenarios Where DynamoDB is a Bad Fit:**
- **Financial / Banking Systems with Complex Transactions:** Systems requiring strict ACID relational joins across customers, branches, accounts, and audits.
- **Reporting & Business Intelligence Dashboards:** Running `SELECT ... GROUP BY ... ORDER BY` across multiple attributes requires full table scans in DynamoDB, which is slow and extremely expensive.
- **Relational Data with Rapidly Evolving Filters:** Use Amazon Aurora / PostgreSQL instead.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is Prometheus and what is Grafana?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Prometheus:
- **What it is:** An open-source systems monitoring and alerting toolkit designed for dynamic environments like Kubernetes.
- **Core Mechanism:** It uses a **pull-based model** (scraping HTTP metrics endpoints like `/metrics` at periodic intervals), stores data in a **time-series database (TSDB)**, and evaluates alert rules using its query language, **PromQL**.

### Grafana:
- **What it is:** An open-source analytics and visualization web platform.
- **Core Mechanism:** Grafana does not store metrics itself; instead, it connects to data sources (Prometheus, CloudWatch, Elasticsearch, MySQL) to query data and render charts, heatmaps, tables, and dashboards.

### In Short:
**Prometheus is the engine that collects, stores, and evaluates metrics**, while **Grafana is the presentation dashboard that visualizes those metrics** for engineers.
</details>

</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If two employees each upload a file with the same name to the same S3 bucket, how can S3 ensure that each user can retrieve and access only their own file?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
There are two primary industry-standard approaches to solve this in AWS S3:

### Method 1: Prefix / Folder Isolation with IAM Policies (Recommended)
Organize the bucket structure by employee ID or username:
```text
my-company-bucket/
├── user-101/report.pdf
└── user-102/report.pdf
```
Enforce this using an IAM Policy using the AWS policy variable `${aws:username}`:
```json
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-bucket/${aws:username}/*"
    }
  ]
}
```
This guarantees user 101 can only see and retrieve objects inside their own folder.

### Method 2: S3 Object Versioning (If using the exact same path)
- If both upload to `s3://my-bucket/report.pdf`, enabling **S3 Versioning** prevents the first file from being overwritten. S3 assigns unique `VersionId` strings to each upload.
- However, to distinguish ownership, object tagging or custom metadata (`x-amz-meta-uploader: user-101`) must be attached on upload, and the retrieval application queries by version and metadata.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between AWS Auto Scaling and load balancers?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
While both work together to build scalable, fault-tolerant architectures, they have fundamentally different roles:

| Feature | AWS Auto Scaling (ASG) | Elastic Load Balancer (ELB / ALB) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Primary Role** | Manages the **number** of compute instances (scale in / scale out). | Distributes incoming network traffic **across** active instances. |
| **Trigger** | Metric thresholds (e.g., average CPU > 70%, request count). | Operates in real-time on every incoming client request. |
| **Health Check Action** | Replaces / terminates unhealthy instances with fresh ones. | Stops routing traffic to unhealthy targets without terminating them. |
| **Layer** | Management layer (Compute provisioning). | Networking layer (OSI Layer 4 / Layer 7). |

### Analogy:
The **Load Balancer** is like a receptionist directing incoming visitors to available service counters. **Auto Scaling** is the manager who opens new service counters when the waiting line gets too long and closes them when traffic subsides.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with GitOps?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have worked with **GitOps** principles and tools (specifically **Argo CD**).

### Core GitOps Principles Implemented:
1. **Git as the Single Source of Truth:** The entire desired state of the infrastructure and Kubernetes applications is declared in Git repositories.
2. **Pull-Based Synchronization:** An in-cluster operator (Argo CD) continuously monitors Git. If a developer merges code or changes an image tag in Git, the operator pulls and applies the changes automatically.
3. **No Direct Cluster Access for CI:** CI systems (Jenkins / GitHub Actions) only build, test, and commit new image tags into the configuration repository; they do not need `kubectl admin` access to the production cluster, improving security.
4. **Automated Drift Detection:** If someone makes an out-of-band manual change in the cluster via `kubectl`, Argo CD detects the drift and can automatically reconcile it back to the Git state.
</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If an application is returning a 503 error, how would you troubleshoot and resolve the issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **503 Service Unavailable** error indicates that the server or load balancer is currently unable to handle the request.

### Step-by-Step Troubleshooting Procedure:

#### 1. Check Load Balancer Target Health:
- Inspect the ALB Target Group health status in AWS Console or via AWS CLI:
  ```bash
  aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn <tg-arn>
  ```
- If all targets are `Unhealthy`, the ALB returns an immediate 503 because there are zero healthy backends to receive traffic.

#### 2. Check Backend Application / Pod Health:
- In Kubernetes, check pod status and logs:
  ```bash
  kubectl get pods -n <namespace>
  kubectl logs -l app=<app-name> -n <namespace> --tail=100
  ```
- Look for pod crashes (`CrashLoopBackOff`), out of memory kills (`OOMKilled`), or failing health check endpoints (`/healthz`).

#### 3. Verify Health Check Configuration:
- Check if the target group health check path, port, or timeout matches the application configuration. If the app takes 10 seconds to respond to `/health`, but the timeout is set to 5 seconds, targets will be marked unhealthy.

#### 4. Check Resource Saturation (CPU / Memory / Connection Limits):
- High CPU/memory usage or exhausted database connection pools can cause the application to stop accepting incoming connections, causing the load balancer connection to time out.
- Solution: Scale out replicas or instances, and increase database connection pool limits.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Given 100+ AWS EC2 instances, how would you automate generating an hourly report of the instances that are in the terminated state?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We can automate this using a Python script with **Boto3**:

### Python Script (`report_terminated_ec2.py`):
```python
import boto3
import csv
from datetime import datetime

def generate_terminated_ec2_report():
    ec2 = boto3.client('ec2', region_name='us-east-1')
    
    # Filter only terminated instances
    response = ec2.describe_instances(
        Filters=[{'Name': 'instance-state-name', 'Values': ['terminated']}]
    )
    
    filename = f"/var/reports/terminated_ec2_{datetime.utcnow().strftime('%Y%m%d_%H%M%S')}.csv"
    
    with open(filename, mode='w', newline='') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(['InstanceId', 'InstanceType', 'LaunchTime', 'StateTransitionReason'])
        
        for reservation in response['Reservations']:
            for inst in reservation['Instances']:
                writer.writerow([
                    inst.get('InstanceId'),
                    inst.get('InstanceType'),
                    str(inst.get('LaunchTime')),
                    inst.get('StateTransitionReason', 'N/A')
                ])
                
    print(f"Report generated: {filename}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    generate_terminated_ec2_report()
```
</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Now that you have the script to list terminated EC2 instances, what will you do with it to make it run every hour?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To make the script run every hour, I will schedule it using an automated scheduler. The standard Linux approach is to configure a **Cron job** on a dedicated management or utility server that has the appropriate IAM role permissions.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How will you make the script execute automatically every hour?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I will register the script inside the Linux user's **crontab** table with the hourly schedule expression `0 * * * *`. This instructs the `cron` daemon to trigger execution at minute 0 of every hour.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Explain step by step how you would set up the script to run on a schedule using a cron job.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Step-by-Step Cron Job Setup:

1. **Place the Script & Make it Executable:**
   ```bash
   sudo mkdir -p /opt/scripts /var/reports
   sudo cp report_terminated_ec2.py /opt/scripts/
   sudo chmod +x /opt/scripts/report_terminated_ec2.py
   ```

2. **Open the Crontab Editor:**
   ```bash
   crontab -e
   ```

3. **Add the Hourly Cron Entry:**
   ```bash
   0 * * * * /usr/bin/python3 /opt/scripts/report_terminated_ec2.py >> /var/log/ec2_report.log 2>&1
   ```
   *Explanation of schedule `0 * * * *`:*
   - `0`: Minute 0
   - `*`: Every hour
   - `*`: Every day of the month
   - `*`: Every month
   - `*`: Every day of the week

4. **Verify the Crontab:**
   ```bash
   crontab -l
   ```
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Apart from a cron job, what other approach could you use to run the EC2 terminated-instance report on a schedule?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Apart from a traditional Linux cron job on an EC2 instance, the recommended modern cloud-native approach is:

1. **AWS Lambda + Amazon EventBridge (CloudWatch Events):**
   - **Serverless & Cost-Effective:** No EC2 instance needs to run 24/7. EventBridge triggers the Lambda function on a schedule rate (`rate(1 hour)`), executes the Python Boto3 logic in seconds, sends the report via SES/Slack, and shuts down.
2. **Kubernetes CronJob:**
   - If running an EKS cluster, define a `CronJob` manifest that spins up a lightweight container hourly, runs the script, and terminates upon completion.
3. **AWS Systems Manager (SSM) Maintenance Windows / State Manager:**
   - Schedules automated runbook executions across EC2 fleets without manual SSH access.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you implement the hourly EC2 terminated-instance report using AWS Lambda?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Step-by-Step Serverless Architecture:

1. **Create an IAM Role for Lambda:**
   - Attach policies allowing `ec2:DescribeInstances`, `cloudwatch:PutMetricData`, and `s3:PutObject` (or `ses:SendEmail`).
2. **Write the Lambda Function Code (Python):**
   ```python
   import boto3
   import json
   from datetime import datetime
   
   def lambda_handler(event, context):
       ec2 = boto3.client('ec2')
       s3 = boto3.client('s3')
       
       res = ec2.describe_instances(
           Filters=[{'Name': 'instance-state-name', 'Values': ['terminated']}]
       )
       
       records = []
       for r in res['Reservations']:
           for i in r['Instances']:
               records.append(f"{i['InstanceId']},{i.get('InstanceType')},{i.get('LaunchTime')}")
               
       content = "InstanceId,InstanceType,LaunchTime
" + "
".join(records)
       
       key = f"reports/terminated_{datetime.utcnow().strftime('%Y%m%d_%H%M%S')}.csv"
       s3.put_object(Bucket='my-audit-reports-bucket', Key=key, Body=content)
       
       return {"status": "success", "file": key}
   ```
3. **Configure Amazon EventBridge Trigger:**
   - Create an EventBridge Rule with Schedule expression: `cron(0 * * * ? *)` or `rate(1 hour)`.
   - Set the Target to the Lambda function.
</details>

**Answer:**
Yes, **Model Context Protocol (MCP)** is an open standard introduced by Anthropic that enables AI models and assistants to securely connect and interface with external tools, systems, and data sources.

### Key Concepts:
1. **Standardized Protocol:** Acts like a "USB-C port for AI apps," standardizing how LLMs communicate with databases, file systems, Git repositories, or APIs.
2. **MCP Host & MCP Server:**
   - **Host (Client):** An AI application or IDE (such as Claude Desktop or Cursor).
   - **Server:** A lightweight service exposing specific tools, resources, and prompts (e.g., a GitHub MCP server, a PostgreSQL MCP server, or an AWS MCP server).
3. **Benefit in DevOps:** Instead of copying and pasting logs or schema manually into a chat window, an MCP server gives the LLM direct, permission-controlled tool access to inspect git repos, run database queries, or query cloud resource state safely.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Design a highly available, fault-tolerant and scalable web application on AWS using services such as EC2, Auto Scaling Groups, Elastic Load Balancing, RDS, S3 and Route 53.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here is the production-grade multi-AZ architecture:

```text
       [ Users / Clients ]
               │
               ▼
       [ Amazon Route 53 ] (DNS + Latency / Failover Routing)
               │
               ▼
  [ Application Load Balancer (ALB) ] (Public Subnets across AZ-1 & AZ-2)
         │                   │
   ┌─────┴───────────────────┴─────┐
   ▼                               ▼
[ EC2 (AZ-1) ]               [ EC2 (AZ-2) ]   (Auto Scaling Group in Private Subnets)
   │                               │
   └───────────────┬───────────────┘
                   │
                   ▼
  [ Amazon RDS Multi-AZ Database ] (Private DB Subnet)
       Primary (AZ-1) ──Sync Replication──► Standby (AZ-2)
                   ▲
                   │ (Static Assets & User Uploads)
             [ Amazon S3 ] + [ CloudFront CDN ]
```

### Key Architectural Pillars:
1. **Route 53:** Serves DNS queries with health checks and routes traffic to the public Application Load Balancer.
2. **Application Load Balancer (ALB):** Spans multiple Availability Zones (public subnets), terminates SSL/TLS, and distributes traffic across EC2 instances.
3. **Auto Scaling Group (ASG):** Deploys EC2 instances across private subnets in at least two AZs. Automatically scales instances in/out based on CPU or request count targets.
4. **Amazon RDS Multi-AZ:** Runs primary database in AZ-1 with synchronous replication to an automated standby replica in AZ-2 for instant failover without data loss.
5. **Amazon S3 + CloudFront:** Serves media, images, and static frontend assets directly to users via global edge locations, offloading traffic from EC2.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Design a real-time data ingestion pipeline on AWS for streaming sensor data that uses Kinesis, Lambda, S3, Glue and Athena.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
This architecture provides a scalable, serverless real-time data ingestion and analytics pipeline:

```text
[ IoT Sensors / Devices ]
          │ (Stream Data)
          ▼
 [ Amazon Kinesis Data Streams ]
          │ (Trigger / Batching)
          ▼
   [ AWS Lambda ] (Data validation, filtering, transformation)
          │
          ▼
   [ Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose ]
          │ (Buffer, convert to Parquet)
          ▼
  [ Amazon S3 Raw / Processed Bucket ]
          ▲
          │ (Cataloging & Schema Discovery)
  [ AWS Glue Data Catalog & Crawler ]
          ▲
          │ (Serverless SQL Queries)
   [ Amazon Athena ]
```

### Workflow Steps:
1. **Ingestion (Kinesis Data Streams):** Sensor devices push real-time event records into Kinesis Data Streams partitioned by sensor ID.
2. **Transformation (AWS Lambda):** Lambda processes streaming batches in real time to clean data, filter corrupted records, and normalize formats.
3. **Buffering & Delivery (Kinesis Data Firehose):** Aggregates data, converts JSON to compressed columnar format (**Apache Parquet**), and flushes it to Amazon S3 partitioned by date (`year=YYYY/month=MM/day=DD/`).
4. **Metadata Cataloging (AWS Glue):** A scheduled Glue Crawler inspects S3 objects to infer the schema and update tables in the **AWS Glue Data Catalog**.
5. **Serverless Analytics (Amazon Athena):** Analysts run standard SQL queries directly against data stored in S3 via Athena with high performance and low query costs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Which AWS service is best suited for storing static website content, backups and data objects: Amazon RDS, Amazon EC2, Amazon S3, or Amazon DynamoDB?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Amazon S3 (Simple Storage Service)** is the best suited service.

### Why:
- **Object Storage Design:** S3 is purpose-built to store and retrieve any amount of unstructured data (images, HTML/CSS/JS files, video, archives, backups, logs) from anywhere.
- **Industry-Leading Durability & Availability:** Offers $99.999999999\%$ (11 9s) of data durability.
- **Cost Efficiency & Lifecycle Rules:** Allows transitioning older backups to colder storage classes (S3 Standard-Infrequent Access, S3 Glacier, Glacier Deep Archive).
- **Comparison:**
  - *Amazon RDS:* Relational database for structured tabular data.
  - *Amazon EC2:* Virtual compute servers; local EBS storage is expensive for static file serving.
  - *Amazon DynamoDB:* NoSQL key-value database capped at 400 KB per item.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the benefits of using AWS load balancers (Elastic Load Balancing)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The key benefits of **AWS Elastic Load Balancing (ELB / ALB / NLB)** are:

1. **High Availability & Fault Tolerance:** Automatically distributes incoming traffic across instances, containers, and IP addresses across multiple Availability Zones. Unhealthy targets are automatically bypassed.
2. **Automatic Elastic Scaling:** Automatically scales its own request handling capacity up and down to match incoming traffic patterns without manual intervention.
3. **Health Monitoring:** Periodically performs Layer 4 or Layer 7 health checks on registered backend targets.
4. **SSL/TLS Termination & Offloading:** Manages certificates via AWS Certificate Manager (ACM), offloading CPU-intensive decryption from backend servers.
5. **Advanced Routing Capabilities (ALB):** Supports path-based routing (`/api` vs `/static`), host-based routing (`app.example.com`), query parameter routing, and HTTP redirect rules (HTTP to HTTPS).
6. **Security Integration:** Natively integrates with **AWS WAF (Web Application Firewall)** to protect against DDoS, SQL injection, and cross-site scripting.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Which AWS services are used to provide high availability and scalability for an application deployed on EC2: Amazon S3, Amazon Auto Scaling, AWS Load Balancer, CloudWatch, or VPC?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The primary services providing high availability and scalability for EC2 applications are:

1. **AWS Load Balancer (ELB/ALB):** Provides **High Availability** by distributing traffic across multiple AZs and directing traffic only to healthy EC2 instances.
2. **Amazon Auto Scaling (ASG):** Provides **Scalability and High Availability** by adding or removing EC2 instances dynamically based on demand and replacing failed or unhealthy instances.
3. **Amazon CloudWatch:** Provides the **Metric Alarms** (e.g., CPU utilization > 70%) that trigger Auto Scaling actions.

*(Note: **VPC** provides the isolated network foundation and multi-AZ subnet layout, while **S3** is object storage, not an EC2 compute scaling service.)*
</details>

**Answer:**
Building a modern, centralized **Data Lake on AWS** typically involves the following architecture:

1. **Storage Layer (Amazon S3):**
   - Acts as the central, scalable storage repository structured into zones:
     - **Raw Zone (Bronze):** Ingests raw data as-is from databases, APIs, and logs.
     - **Cleaned/Transformed Zone (Silver):** Filtered, deduplicated, and converted to columnar Parquet format.
     - **Curated Zone (Gold):** Aggregated data ready for business intelligence and ML models.
2. **Security & Governance (AWS Lake Formation):**
   - Centrally defines fine-grained access control policies (table-level, row-level, and column-level security) across data assets.
3. **Cataloging & Metadata (AWS Glue Data Catalog):**
   - Glue Crawlers inspect S3 files, infer schemas, and maintain unified metadata tables.
4. **Processing & Querying Layer:**
   - **Amazon Athena:** Serverless interactive ad-hoc SQL querying.
   - **Amazon EMR (Spark / Hadoop):** Large-scale batch processing and distributed transformations.
   - **Amazon Redshift Spectrum:** Querying S3 data directly from the enterprise data warehouse.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In the AWS shared responsibility model, what are the customer's responsibilities (as opposed to AWS's)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Under the AWS Shared Responsibility Model:
- **AWS is responsible for "Security OF the Cloud":** Physical data centers, hardware, host virtualization layer, facilities, power, networking infrastructure, and managed service runtimes.
- **The Customer is responsible for "Security IN the Cloud":**

### Specific Customer Responsibilities:
1. **Customer Data:** Data encryption (at-rest via KMS and in-transit via TLS/SSL) and backup policies.
2. **IAM & Access Management:** User credentials, MFA enforcement, least-privilege IAM policies, and role definitions.
3. **Operating System Configuration & Patching:** For unmanaged compute (EC2 instances), OS updates, security patches, and SSH key management.
4. **Network & Firewall Configuration:** Configuring Security Groups, Network ACLs, and VPC routing tables.
5. **Application Code:** Securing application code, patching libraries, preventing vulnerabilities (SQLi, XSS), and configuring AWS WAF.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Design a scalable AWS architecture using EC2 instances, a load balancer and Auto Scaling.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Step-by-Step Architecture Design:

1. **VPC & Subnet Layout:**
   - Create a VPC spanning two Availability Zones.
   - Provision 2 Public Subnets (for the Application Load Balancer and NAT Gateways).
   - Provision 2 Private Subnets (for the EC2 application instances).
2. **Launch Template:**
   - Define a Launch Template specifying the Golden AMI, EC2 instance type (`t3.medium`), IAM Instance Profile, and user-data initialization script.
   - Attach a Security Group allowing inbound traffic on port 8080 **only from the ALB security group**.
3. **Application Load Balancer (ALB):**
   - Deploy ALB in the public subnets.
   - Configure a Target Group with HTTP health check path `/health`.
   - Setup HTTPS listener (port 443) using an ACM SSL certificate and forward to the target group.
4. **Auto Scaling Group (ASG):**
   - Attach the Launch Template and register targets with the ALB Target Group.
   - Define scaling limits: `Min: 2`, `Desired: 2`, `Max: 8`.
   - Attach target tracking policy: Maintain average CPU utilization at 65%.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you design an AWS architecture that focuses on both cost optimization and high performance?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Achieving both cost optimization and high performance requires leveraging modern cloud patterns across compute, storage, caching, and purchasing models:

### 1. Compute & Workload Optimization:
- **Right-Sizing:** Monitor CloudWatch and AWS Compute Optimizer to select appropriate instance families (e.g., Graviton/ARM-based `c7g` / `m7g` instances, which provide up to 20% lower cost and 40% better performance over x86).
- **Spot & On-Demand Mix:** In Auto Scaling Groups, use On-Demand instances for baseline capacity and Spot Instances (with capacity-optimized allocation) for burstable scale-out.
- **Savings Plans / Reserved Instances (RIs):** Commit to Compute Savings Plans for predictable baseline workloads to save up to 72%.

### 2. Caching & Offloading:
- **Edge Caching with CloudFront:** Cache static and dynamic content at edge locations to eliminate origin EC2/ALB load and reduce expensive data transfer costs.
- **In-Memory Caching (Redis/ElastiCache):** Cache repeated database query results to deliver sub-millisecond response times and reduce database instance sizes.

### 3. Storage Lifecycle Management:
- Use **S3 Intelligent-Tiering** to automatically move objects between frequent and infrequent access tiers without performance penalties or retrieval fees.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Which Terraform built-in functions are available for data formatting, condition checks, mathematical operations and other data manipulations, and how are they used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform provides a rich set of built-in functions categorized by purpose:

### 1. String & Data Formatting:
- `format("app-%s-%02d", var.env, count.index)`: Formats strings using printf-style specifiers.
- `jsonencode(map)` / `jsondecode(str)`: Encodes or decodes JSON structures (great for IAM policy generation).
- `yamlencode()` / `yamldecode()`: Parses and renders YAML.
- `join(",", list)` and `split(",", str)`: Concatenates or breaks strings.

### 2. Collection & Manipulation Functions:
- `merge(map1, map2)`: Combines multiple maps into one (e.g., combining common tags with resource tags).
- `lookup(map, key, default)`: Safely retrieves a map value with a fallback.
- `concat(list1, list2)`: Combines multiple lists.
- `flatten(list_of_lists)`: Flattens nested arrays into a single-level list.

### 3. Conditional & Logical Functions:
- `try(expression, fallback)`: Evaluates an expression and returns the fallback if an error occurs.
- `can(expression)`: Returns true if the expression evaluates without error.
- `coalesce(val1, val2, ...)`: Returns the first non-empty, non-null value.

### 4. Mathematical & Encoding:
- `max()`, `min()`, `length()`: Calculates extremes or counts elements.
- `base64encode(str)` / `base64decode(str)`: Encodes/decodes user data scripts.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Explain your strategy for structuring and using Terraform modules.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
My strategy is built around the **Modular Layered Architecture** separating reusable modules from environment root modules:

### 1. Directory Structure:
```text
terraform-repo/
├── modules/                      # Reusable Child Modules
│   ├── vpc/
│   │   ├── main.tf
│   │   ├── variables.tf
│   │   └── outputs.tf
│   ├── eks/
│   └── rds/
└── environments/                 # Root Modules per environment
    ├── dev/
    │   ├── main.tf               # Calls ../../modules/*
    │   ├── backend.tf            # S3 bucket + DynamoDB lock table
    │   ├── terraform.tfvars      # Environment specific variable inputs
    │   └── versions.tf
    ├── qa/
    └── prod/
```

### 2. Key Principles:
- **Single Responsibility:** Each module manages one logical boundary (e.g., Networking, Compute, Database).
- **Loose Coupling via Inputs & Outputs:** Child modules never hardcode values; they declare required inputs in `variables.tf` and expose resources via `outputs.tf` (e.g., passing `module.vpc.vpc_id` into `module.eks`).
- **Independent State Files:** Each environment (`dev`, `prod`) maintains its own remote state file to isolate blast radius.
- **Semantic Versioning:** In enterprise setups, child modules reside in dedicated Git repos and root modules pin versions: `source = "git::https://github.com/org/terraform-aws-vpc.git?ref=v2.1.0"`.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do conditional expressions work in Terraform, and how would you use them?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Conditional expressions in Terraform use the ternary operator syntax:
```text
condition ? true_val : false_val
```

### Common Production Use Cases:

#### 1. Conditional Resource Creation (Toggling Resources):
Use `count` with a boolean variable to create a resource only in specific environments (e.g., creating a bastion host only in Dev/QA):
```hcl
resource "aws_instance" "bastion" {
  count         = var.enable_bastion ? 1 : 0
  ami           = "ami-12345678"
  instance_type = "t3.micro"
}
```

#### 2. Conditional Values for Configuration Attributes:
Set different instance sizes or replica counts based on the environment:
```hcl
locals {
  instance_type = var.environment == "prod" ? "m5.large" : "t3.medium"
  multi_az      = var.environment == "prod" ? true : false
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "db" {
  instance_class = local.instance_type
  multi_az       = local.multi_az
  # ...
}
```
</details>

data "aws_instance" "app_server" {

    name   = "tag:Name"
    values = ["production-web-server"]
  }

  filter {
    name   = "instance-state-name"
    values = ["running"]
  }
}

```hcl
output "server_ip" {
  value = data.aws_instance.app_server.private_ip
}
```

*Note: If you need to search multiple instances, use the **`aws_instances`** (plural) data source, which returns a list of instance IDs.*

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What Terraform infrastructure deployment strategy would you use to provision a VPC, an RDS database and EC2 instances?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The recommended deployment strategy is **Dependency-Driven Layered Provisioning**:

### 1. Phased Architecture & State Separation:
Do not put VPC, RDS, and EC2 into a single monolithic state file. Split them into layers:
- **Layer 1: Network Layer (VPC, Subnets, NAT Gateways, Route Tables)**: Changes very rarely.
- **Layer 2: Database Layer (RDS Subnet Group, Security Groups, Multi-AZ RDS)**: Changes infrequently; holds stateful data.
- **Layer 3: Application Compute Layer (EC2, Launch Templates, Auto Scaling, ALB)**: Changes frequently with application releases.

### 2. Passing Data Between Layers:
Pass outputs from lower layers to higher layers using:
- **Method A: `terraform_remote_state` Data Source:** The EC2 layer reads the VPC and Subnet IDs directly from the VPC remote state in S3.
- **Method B: AWS SSM Parameter Store:** The VPC module writes IDs to SSM (e.g., `/prod/network/vpc_id`), and compute modules read them using `data "aws_ssm_parameter"`.

### 3. Benefits:
- **Minimal Blast Radius:** Modifying or destroying an application server never risks accidentally modifying the VPC or deleting the database.
- **Speed:** Faster `terraform plan` execution because only the targeted layer is evaluated.
</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● In a shell script function, how do you access the first argument passed to that function?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In a shell script function, you access the first argument using positional parameter **`$1`**.

### Example:
```bash
#!/bin/bash

# Define function
greet_user() {
    local username="$1"
    echo "Welcome to the system, ${username}!"
}

# Call function with an argument
greet_user "Nagaraj"
```

### Important Rule:
Inside a function, `$1`, `$2`, etc., refer to the arguments passed **to that specific function**, not the arguments passed to the outer shell script.
</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between an environment variable and a shell variable in a Linux shell?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The fundamental difference is **scope and inheritance to child processes**:

| Feature | Shell Variable (Local Variable) | Environment Variable (Global Variable) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Scope** | Available only within the current shell session. | Inherited by any child process or subshell spawned from the current shell. |
| **Creation** | `VAR="value"` | `export VAR="value"` or `declare -x VAR="value"` |
| **Visibility** | Seen by `set`, not seen by `env` or `printenv`. | Seen by both `env` and `printenv`. |
| **Child Processes** | Scripts or subprocesses executed cannot see it. | Subprocesses and commands inherit and read it. |

### Concrete Demonstration:
```bash
# Shell Variable:
MY_LOCAL="hello"
python3 -c "import os; print(os.getenv('MY_LOCAL'))"
# Output: None

# Environment Variable:
export MY_ENV="world"
python3 -c "import os; print(os.getenv('MY_ENV'))"
# Output: world
```
</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Given a directory containing multiple log files where error messages are mixed with other information, how would you write a script to identify, count and list all the unique error types across these files?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We can accomplish this using standard Linux command-line utilities (`grep`, `awk`, `sort`, `uniq`) or a dedicated Bash script.

### Method 1: One-Liner Command
Assuming errors follow the format `ERROR: <ErrorDescription>` or `[ERROR] <Reason>`:
```bash
grep -h -o -E "ERROR: [^,
]+" /var/log/app/*.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -nr
```
*Explanation:*
- `grep -h`: Searches without printing file names.
- `-o`: Outputs only the matching error text, not the entire line.
- `-E`: Uses extended regular expressions.
- `sort | uniq -c`: Groups identical error messages and prefixes with the count.
- `sort -nr`: Sorts by count numerically descending (most frequent error first).

### Method 2: Comprehensive Shell Script (`summarize_errors.sh`)
```bash
#!/bin/bash

LOG_DIR="/var/log/app"

echo "==============================================="
echo "   Unique Error Summary in: $LOG_DIR"
echo "==============================================="

# Find all .log files, search for ERROR lines, extract error type, sort and count
find "$LOG_DIR" -type f -name "*.log" -exec grep -h "ERROR" {} +   | awk -F'ERROR: ' '{print $2}'   | sort   | uniq -c   | sort -rn   | awk '{count=$1; $1=""; printf "Count: %-6s | Error: %s
", count, $0}'

echo "==============================================="
```
</details>

</details>

</details>
