#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you design a complete AWS environment for a Kubernetes-hosted application, including the traffic flow from the user to the cluster, the placement of the cluster, the networking setup, and how the cluster and traffic flow are secured?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Here is the complete end-to-end secure AWS architecture:

```text
[ Users on Internet ]
         │
         ▼
[ Amazon Route 53 ] (DNS resolution with Alias record)
         │
         ▼
[ AWS WAF ] (Rate limiting, SQLi, XSS, IP reputation rules)
         │
         ▼
[ Internet-Facing ALB ] (Deployed in Public Subnets across 2+ AZs, SSL termination via ACM)
         │
         │ (Traffic forwarded securely across VPC boundary)
         ▼
[ Amazon EKS Cluster ]
   ├── Control Plane (AWS-managed, Private API Endpoint)
   └── Worker Nodes (EC2 / Fargate in Private Subnets across 2+ AZs)
         │
         ▼
   [ Ingress Controller / Pods ]
         ├── Pod Security: Non-root containers, read-only root filesystems
         ├── Network: Calico / AWS VPC CNI NetworkPolicies restricting namespace traffic
         └── Identity: IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA) for least-privilege AWS access
```

### Key Components:
1. **Networking Setup:** Multi-AZ VPC with Public Subnets (ALB & NAT Gateways) and Private Subnets (EKS worker nodes and backend databases). No worker node has a public IP.
2. **Traffic Flow:**
   - User hits DNS -> Route 53 resolves Alias to public ALB.
   - ALB inspects traffic via AWS WAF and terminates HTTPS (ACM certificate).
   - ALB forwards traffic directly to worker nodes / pods on their private IPs (using AWS Load Balancer Controller with IP target mode).
3. **Security Layers:**
   - **Perimeter:** AWS WAF + Route 53 + ALB Security Groups (allow 443 from `0.0.0.0/0`).
   - **Node Security:** Node Security Groups allow ingress only from the ALB security group.
   - **Pod-to-Pod Security:** Kubernetes `NetworkPolicy` blocks unauthorized cross-namespace communication.
   - **IAM Security:** IRSA attaches fine-grained IAM roles to Kubernetes ServiceAccounts so pods query AWS APIs without static keys.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does the load balancer forward traffic to the applications running in the Kubernetes cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
There are two primary modes for forwarding traffic from an AWS Load Balancer into Kubernetes:

### 1. IP Mode (Recommended with AWS Load Balancer Controller):
- The AWS Load Balancer Controller configures the ALB Target Group with `target_type: ip`.
- Because the **AWS VPC CNI** assigns real VPC IP addresses directly to Kubernetes pods, the ALB forwards traffic **directly to the Pod IP**, bypassing kube-proxy and extra network hops (`NodePort`). This provides lowest latency and optimal load distribution.

### 2. Instance Mode (Traditional NodePort):
- The ALB Target Group registers the EKS worker nodes as targets on a high port (`NodePort`, e.g., 30000-32767).
- Traffic hits an EC2 worker node, and `kube-proxy` (via iptables/IPVS) routes the packet internally to the node where the destination pod is running.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● An application running in a Kubernetes cluster returns a proper 200 response when accessed directly inside the pod, but returns 502 Bad Gateway when accessed through the user-facing DNS name. What could be the reasons, and what steps would you follow to debug and resolve the issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **502 Bad Gateway** from a user-facing URL indicates that the edge reverse proxy or load balancer (ALB / Ingress Controller) received an invalid response or connection reset when attempting to communicate with the backend upstream.

### Potential Causes:
1. **Target Group Health Check Failing:** The ALB Target Group marks all pods unhealthy because the health check path or port is wrong, or the pod takes too long to respond.
2. **Security Group Restriction:** The security group on the worker nodes does not allow inbound traffic from the ALB security group on the target pod/node port.
3. **Port Mismatch:** The Kubernetes Service `targetPort` does not match the actual port the container is listening on.
4. **Keep-Alive / Connection Timeout:** The application web server keep-alive timeout is shorter than the ALB idle timeout, causing the application to close the TCP connection while the ALB is still trying to reuse it.

### Step-by-Step Debugging Steps:
1. **Check Target Health in AWS Console / CLI:**
   ```bash
   aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn <arn>
   ```
   Check reason: `Target.FailedHealthChecks`, `Target.Timeout`, or `Target.ResponseCodeMismatch`.
2. **Inspect Ingress and Service Manifests:**
   Verify `service.spec.ports.targetPort` matches the container's `containerPort`.
3. **Test Ingress Controller Directly:**
   Curl the node/service IP from inside the VPC or run:
   ```bash
   kubectl port-forward svc/<service-name> 8080:<port>
   curl -I http://localhost:8080/healthz
   ```
4. **Check Security Group Rules:**
   Ensure worker node security group permits ingress from the ALB security group.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If every user is getting the 502 Bad Gateway error, would you ask every user to clear their cache? What would you do instead to address a CloudFront or DNS caching issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No, absolutely not.** Asking end-users to clear their browser cache is bad user experience and does not fix a server-side 502 infrastructure issue.

### What to Do Instead:
1. **Fix the Backend Root Cause First:** 502 originates from the backend or load balancer. Verify the load balancer targets are healthy and responding.
2. **Invalidate CloudFront Cache Immediately:**
   - If Amazon CloudFront cached a 502 error response (negative TTL caching), create an **invalidation** via AWS CLI or Console:
     ```bash
     aws cloudfront create-invalidation --distribution-id <dist-id> --paths "/*"
     ```
   - This immediately purges cached error objects from all edge locations worldwide without requiring any client actions.
3. **Adjust CloudFront Error Caching Minimum TTL:**
   - In CloudFront Distribution settings, set the **Error Caching Minimum TTL** for 502 / 503 / 504 errors to 0 or 1 second (default is often 10 seconds to 5 minutes), preventing temporary glitches from being cached.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If CloudFront is not being used in the architecture, how would you debug the 502 Bad Gateway error when the application works fine inside the pod?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If CloudFront is not in front, the request path is: **User -> Route 53 -> ALB -> Pod**.

### Debugging Steps:
1. **Check ALB Access Logs:**
   - Enable and inspect ALB access logs in S3. Look at fields: `elb_status_code` vs `target_status_code`.
   - If `elb_status_code` is 502 and `target_status_code` is `-`, the ALB could not establish a TCP handshake or SSL handshake with the backend target.
2. **Inspect Target Group Health & Status Details:**
   - Run `aws elbv2 describe-target-health`. If status is `Unhealthy`, inspect the exact error reason string.
3. **Verify Security Groups & Network ACLs:**
   - Check if traffic from the ALB's private subnet IPs can reach the worker nodes on the target port.
4. **Check Keep-Alive Timeout Settings:**
   - In Node.js, Go, or Python/Uvicorn, ensure `keepAliveTimeout` on the application server is configured to be **greater than the ALB idle timeout** (e.g., set app server timeout to 65 seconds if ALB timeout is 60 seconds).
5. **Check Pod Readiness Probes:**
   - Verify that the pod's readiness probe is passing so Kubernetes keeps the endpoint registered.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If the 502 Bad Gateway error is caused by the Application Load Balancer (ALB), what would you check inside the ALB, and what kinds of ALB issues can cause a 502 error?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### What to Check Inside the ALB:
1. **Target Group Health Check Configuration:**
   - Check the Health Check Path (e.g., `/health` vs `/`), Port, Protocol (HTTP vs HTTPS), and expected HTTP matcher codes (`200` vs `200-399`).
2. **Target Registration:**
   - Check whether targets are registered using correct Private IPs and Ports.
3. **ALB Attributes & Idle Timeout:**
   - Check the connection idle timeout (default 60s).
4. **SSL / TLS Certificate on Backend (if end-to-end TLS is enabled):**
   - If ALB forwards HTTPS to the backend, an untrusted certificate or protocol mismatch causes an immediate 502.

### Common ALB Issues Causing 502:
- **Connection Refused:** ALB tries to connect to target IP:port, but nothing is listening (pod crashed or wrong port mapped).
- **Target Connection Timeout:** The target does not complete TCP three-way handshake within the connect timeout.
- **Premature TCP Reset (RST):** The backend closes or resets the connection while the ALB is waiting for response headers.
- **Malformed HTTP Response:** Backend returns headers that violate standard HTTP specifications (e.g., duplicate Content-Length headers).
</details>

#### 【 SECURITY 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If someone runs a script that sends 2,000 to 3,000 requests per minute to your application infrastructure, which can only handle about 1,000 requests at a time, and this brings the application down, how can you resolve this issue or restrict the offending user?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To resolve this issue and protect the application from abusive spikes or DoS attacks, implement a multi-layered defense:

### 1. Implement AWS WAF Rate-Based Rules (Immediate Fix):
- Attach **AWS WAF** to the Application Load Balancer or CloudFront distribution.
- Configure a **Rate-based rule** that limits requests from any single IP address (e.g., maximum 500 requests per 5-minute window). When exceeded, the offending IP is automatically blocked or served an HTTP 429 / CAPTCHA response at the edge.

### 2. Restrict the Offending IP via Network ACL / WAF IP Set:
- Check ALB access logs to identify the attacker's IP address.
- Add the offending IP to a **WAF Block List** or **VPC Network ACL (NACL)** with a `DENY` rule to drop traffic before it touches the load balancer.

### 3. Application / API Gateway Rate Limiting:
- If using Amazon API Gateway or Nginx Ingress, configure rate limiting and throttling (e.g., Nginx `limit_req_zone`).

### 4. Auto Scaling & HPA Buffer:
- Configure Kubernetes Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA) and EC2 Auto Scaling to automatically spin up additional replicas during traffic spikes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What mechanism does AWS WAF use to restrict a particular user or IP address that is sending an excessive number of requests?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS WAF uses **Rate-Based Rule Statements**:

### How It Works:
1. **Evaluation Window:** AWS WAF tracks the number of requests originating from each client IP address across a rolling evaluation window (configurable to 1, 2, 5, or 10 minutes).
2. **Threshold Limit:** You set a request threshold limit (e.g., 500 requests).
3. **Aggregation Keys:** By default, it aggregates by **Source IP address**. You can also aggregate by forwarded IP headers (`X-Forwarded-For`), HTTP headers, or custom query keys (e.g., user account / API token).
4. **Automated Action:** As soon as an IP exceeds the configured limit within the window, WAF immediately triggers the configured action:
   - **Block:** Returns HTTP 403 Forbidden.
   - **Captcha:** Prompts the user with a puzzle to prove human identity.
   - **Count:** Logs the anomaly for monitoring without blocking.
5. **Auto-Recovery:** When the request rate drops below the threshold, WAF automatically unblocks the IP without manual intervention.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How does Amazon Route 53 route traffic to a load balancer, and what routing policy and record types would you use for it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Amazon Route 53 routes traffic to a load balancer using **Alias Records**.

### Record Type:
- **Record Type:** **A Record** (for IPv4) or **AAAA Record** (for IPv6) with the **`Alias = Yes`** toggle enabled.
- **Target:** Pointed directly to the DNS name of the Application Load Balancer (e.g., `my-alb-123456.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com`).

### Routing Policies:
1. **Simple Routing:** Directs all incoming traffic to a single resource (standard for single-region applications).
2. **Weighted Routing:** Distributes traffic across multiple endpoints according to assigned percentages (ideal for canary testing or blue/green deployments).
3. **Latency-based Routing:** Directs users to the AWS region that provides the lowest network latency.
4. **Failover Routing:** Directs traffic to a primary load balancer in Region A, automatically failing over to Region B if Route 53 health checks detect an outage.
5. **Geolocation / Geoproximity Routing:** Routes traffic based on the geographic location of the requesting client.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which type of DNS record would you create in Route 53 to route traffic to the load balancer?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You create an **A (Address) Record** configured as an **Alias Record**:
- **Name:** `app.example.com`
- **Type:** `A`
- **Alias:** `Yes`
- **Route traffic to:** Alias to Application and Classic Load Balancer
- **Region:** The AWS Region where the ALB is hosted (e.g., `us-east-1`)
- **Target:** Select your specific ALB from the dropdown list.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is a DNS A record?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **DNS A Record (Address Record)** is the most fundamental DNS record type. It maps a human-readable domain or subdomain name (such as `example.com`) directly to an **IPv4 address** (such as `192.0.2.1`).

When a client queries a domain name, the DNS resolver returns the 32-bit IPv4 address so the client's browser can initiate a TCP connection with that server.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Since an A record maps a name to an IPv4 address, does a load balancer provide an IP address that you can add to the A record?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No, an AWS Application Load Balancer does not provide a single static IP address.** Instead, an ALB provides a **DNS name** (e.g., `dualstack.my-alb.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com`).

### Why:
Because AWS dynamically scales the ALB nodes in and out across Availability Zones as traffic fluctuates, the underlying IP addresses of the ALB nodes change frequently.

### How Route 53 Solves This:
Route 53 provides **Alias Records** (an AWS-proprietary extension). When you configure an `A` record as an Alias to an ALB, Route 53 internally recognizes the ALB DNS name, tracks its dynamically changing IPs, and resolves client DNS queries directly to the current valid IP addresses without incurring additional DNS query fees.
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you need a static IP address for a load balancer in AWS, how would you get one?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If static IP addresses are strictly required (e.g., for legacy client whitelisting), there are two primary methods:

### Method 1: Use an AWS Network Load Balancer (NLB) (Recommended)
- Unlike an ALB, a **Network Load Balancer (Layer 4)** allows you to assign a dedicated **Elastic IP (EIP)** to each subnet/AZ during creation.
- If you need Layer 7 HTTP routing, you can set up the NLB with static IPs in front, and configure it to forward traffic to an internal ALB.

### Method 2: AWS Global Accelerator with ALB
- Attach **AWS Global Accelerator** in front of your Application Load Balancer.
- Global Accelerator provides **two static Anycast public IPv4 addresses** that route traffic into the AWS global private network directly to your ALB.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If an Application Load Balancer could provide a static IP, why do people not use static IPs with it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
People do not use static IPs directly with ALBs because of how ALBs achieve high availability and massive elastic scaling:

1. **Dynamic Scaling:** An ALB scales horizontally by launching or terminating internal load-balancer compute nodes across multiple AZs in response to traffic surges.
2. **IP Rotation:** As nodes scale up, down, or are replaced during AWS internal maintenance, their IP addresses change.
3. **Availability Zone Resilience:** Relying on hardcoded static IPs bypasses AWS's automated multi-AZ DNS failover. If an entire AZ experiences issues, Route 53 automatically withdraws that AZ's IPs from the DNS response, preserving application uptime.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● When your application consumes a third-party client's APIs, access initially works but is then blocked, probably by a WAF set up on their side. How would you resolve this issue?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When calling a third-party API and getting blocked by their WAF (usually HTTP 403 Forbidden or 429 Too Many Requests):

### Steps to Resolve:
1. **Analyze Error Codes & Response Headers:**
   - Check if response returns `429 Too Many Requests` (rate limiting) or `403 Forbidden` (IP reputation / missing User-Agent / WAF rule trigger).
2. **Contact the Third-Party Vendor:**
   - Coordinate with the vendor's API support team to confirm the blocking reason (e.g., automated rate-limit trigger, lack of whitelisting, or missing required authorization headers).
3. **Provide Outbound Static IP for Whitelisting:**
   - Share your AWS **NAT Gateway Elastic IP (EIP)** so they can add it to their WAF IP Whitelist / Allowed IP Set.
4. **Implement Client-Side Throttling & Backoff:**
   - In application code, implement exponential backoff with jitter and a client-side rate limiter to ensure request volume does not exceed their agreed quota.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If your application continuously hits a third-party API from a single IP address, what is needed from your side so that they do not block you?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
From our side, we need:
1. **Dedicated Static Public IP (Elastic IP on NAT Gateway):** Ensure all outbound traffic exits through a known, predictable IP address.
2. **IP Whitelisting Agreement:** Formally request the third party to add our Elastic IP to their firewall/WAF allowlist.
3. **Proper API Authentication & Headers:** Send valid API Keys, OAuth tokens, and a distinct, recognizable `User-Agent` string (many WAFs block generic Python/curl user agents).
4. **Rate Limiting & Concurrency Control:** Enforce request throttling inside the application (e.g., maximum 10 requests/second) matching the vendor's SLA.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which IP address would you share with the third-party vendor so that they can whitelist your application's traffic?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You share the **Elastic IP (EIP) address attached to your AWS NAT Gateway**.

You should **never** share internal private IP addresses (e.g., `10.0.x.x` or `172.31.x.x`) because private IPs are non-routable over the public internet and cannot be recognized by external third parties.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does an application running in a private subnet access the internet, and which IP would need to be whitelisted by the third party?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### How Outbound Internet Access Works:
1. The application pod runs on an EC2 worker node in a **Private Subnet** (having only a private IP).
2. The private subnet's Route Table contains a default route: `0.0.0.0/0 -> nat-gateway-id`.
3. Outbound packets travel to the **NAT Gateway** located in the **Public Subnet**.
4. The NAT Gateway performs **Source Network Address Translation (SNAT)**: It replaces the source private IP with its own public **Elastic IP (EIP)** and forwards the request out to the internet through the Internet Gateway (IGW).
5. When the third-party server receives the request, it sees the connection originating from the **NAT Gateway's Elastic IP**.

Therefore, the **NAT Gateway's Elastic IP** is the exact IP that must be whitelisted by the third party.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● In a Kubernetes cluster with two namespaces, where a frontend service runs in namespace production-one and a backend service runs in namespace production-two, will the frontend service be able to connect to the backend service?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes**, by default in Kubernetes, all pods and services across different namespaces can freely communicate with each other over the cluster network, provided no restrictive **`NetworkPolicy`** has been applied to block cross-namespace traffic.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would the frontend service in one namespace connect to the backend service in a different namespace?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The frontend service connects to the backend service using the **Fully Qualified Domain Name (FQDN)** resolved by Kubernetes internal DNS (**CoreDNS**).

Inside the same namespace, an application can simply call `http://backend-service:8080`. But across different namespaces, CoreDNS requires appending the target namespace name.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What service name or DNS address should the frontend service use to call the backend service that resides in a different namespace?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The frontend service should use the Kubernetes FQDN format:
```text
<service-name>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local
```

### Specific Example:
- **Backend Service Name:** `backend-service`
- **Backend Namespace:** `production-two`
- **Target Port:** `8080`

The address to call is:
```text
http://backend-service.production-two.svc.cluster.local:8080
```
*(Or the shorthand `http://backend-service.production-two:8080`, as CoreDNS search domains will automatically resolve it).*
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● During a production hotfix deployment using Kubernetes rolling updates, a new pod takes about 2 minutes to become ready, and users receive failed responses during that time because the old pod has already been terminated. How can you handle this scenario to avoid downtime?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
This downtime occurs because the Deployment does not know the application requires 2 minutes to initialize, so it prematurely terminates old pods and routes traffic to the new pod before it is actually ready.

### How to Resolve and Eliminate Downtime:
1. **Configure a `readinessProbe` with Initial Delay / Thresholds:**
   Define a readiness probe pointing to an application health check endpoint. Kubernetes will **not** route traffic to the new pod and will **not** terminate old pods until the readiness probe returns HTTP 200.
2. **Use a `startupProbe` for Slow-Starting Containers:**
   Configure a `startupProbe` with `failureThreshold: 30` and `periodSeconds: 5` (allowing up to 150 seconds for startup). During this time, liveness and readiness checks are disabled.
3. **Configure RollingUpdate Deployment Strategy:**
   ```yaml
   strategy:
     type: RollingUpdate
     rollingUpdate:
       maxUnavailable: 0
       maxSurge: 1
   ```
   Setting `maxUnavailable: 0` guarantees that Kubernetes will never terminate an existing healthy pod until the new replacement pod has completely started and passed its readiness checks.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If Kubernetes forwards traffic to a new pod even before the application in it is actually running properly, what can you use to prevent users from hitting the not-yet-ready pod?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You use a **Readiness Probe (`readinessProbe`)**.

### How Readiness Probes Prevent Traffic Forwarding:
1. By default, when a container process starts, Kubernetes marks the pod as `Running` and adds its IP to the Service's `Endpoints` object.
2. When a `readinessProbe` is defined, Kubernetes keeps the pod out of the Service Endpoints list (status `NotReady`).
3. Only after the readiness probe succeeds (e.g., returns HTTP 200 on `/healthz`) does Kubernetes add the pod to the Endpoints list and begin routing incoming user traffic to it.
4. If the probe fails at any time during operation, Kubernetes immediately removes the pod from the Endpoints list without killing the container.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you set up a startup probe in Kubernetes, and what criteria does it use to decide that the application is running fine and traffic can be transferred to the new pod?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Manifest Example:
```yaml
spec:
  containers:
  - name: my-app
    image: myrepo/app:1.0
    startupProbe:
      httpGet:
        path: /healthz
        port: 8080
      initialDelaySeconds: 10
      periodSeconds: 5
      failureThreshold: 30    # 30 * 5s = 150s max startup budget
    readinessProbe:
      httpGet:
        path: /ready
        port: 8080
      periodSeconds: 5
```

### Criteria Used to Decide Success:
1. **Probe Check Mechanism:** Executes an HTTP GET request (`httpGet`), a TCP socket check (`tcpSocket`), or a container command (`exec`).
2. **Success Criteria:** For HTTP GET, any HTTP status code $\ge 200$ and $< 400$ is considered a success.
3. **Transition to Normal Operation:** Once the startup probe succeeds **just once**, it disables itself completely for the life of the container, and hands over monitoring to the `readinessProbe` and `livenessProbe`.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● An application on a new Kubernetes cluster handles up to 300 requests but restarts when traffic spikes to 500-600 requests. What would you set up so that the application can handle sudden traffic spikes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The restarts occur either because memory exceeds `resources.limits.memory` (causing the Linux kernel to terminate the container via **OOMKilled - Exit Code 137**) or high CPU causes liveness probe timeouts.

### What to Set Up to Handle Spikes:
1. **Increase Container Memory & CPU Limits:**
   - Increase `resources.limits.memory` and `resources.requests.cpu` to give the application enough headroom for bursts.
2. **Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA):**
   - Automatically scale out the number of pod replicas (e.g., from 3 to 15 pods) when average CPU or memory utilization exceeds 65-70%.
3. **Cluster Autoscaler / Karpenter:**
   - Ensure the underlying Kubernetes nodes automatically scale out if new pods cannot fit on existing worker nodes.
4. **Tune Liveness Probe Timeouts:**
   - Ensure `timeoutSeconds` is sufficient so that temporary CPU load spikes don't trigger false restarts.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If a Horizontal Pod Autoscaler is configured and an existing pod restarts under load but new pods do not come up, what could be the reasons?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Common Reasons Why HPA Fails to Scale:
1. **Metrics Server Not Installed / Failing:** HPA relies on the Kubernetes **Metrics Server**. If the Metrics Server is missing or unhealthy, `kubectl get hpa` will show `<unknown>/70%`, and HPA cannot calculate scaling.
2. **Missing `resources.requests` in Pod Spec:** HPA cannot compute percentage-based targets if `spec.containers[].resources.requests.cpu` is omitted.
3. **Scale Target Max Replicas Reached:** The HPA may already have reached its configured `maxReplicas`.
4. **Sudden Memory OOMKill Before HPA Evaluates:** Memory leaks can crash pods instantly (within seconds). Since HPA evaluation period is every 15 seconds, the pod is killed by the OOM killer before HPA triggers a scale-up.
5. **Cluster Node Resource Exhaustion:** HPA might have created the pod objects, but they are stuck in `Pending` state because the worker nodes are out of CPU/memory.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does Kubernetes obtain CPU and memory usage metrics for the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler to work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Kubernetes gathers resource metrics through the following architecture:

1. **cAdvisor (Container Advisor):** Embedded directly inside the **Kubelet** daemon running on every worker node. It collects resource usage (CPU, memory, filesystem, network) from the Linux kernel cgroups for every running container.
2. **Metrics Server:** A cluster-wide aggregator that regularly polls each node's Kubelet/cAdvisor summary API (`/stats/summary`).
3. **Metrics API (`metrics.k8s.io`):** Metrics Server exposes these aggregated metrics through the Kubernetes API aggregator.
4. **HPA Controller:** The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler queries `metrics.k8s.io` every 15 seconds to evaluate replica targets against current usage.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If a new pod is stuck in the ContainerCreating or pending state and the events show that no node is available, what other reasons could cause this besides affinity or anti-affinity rules?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Besides node affinity/anti-affinity rules, other common causes include:

1. **Node Resource Starvation (Insufficient CPU / Memory):** Total `requests.cpu` or `requests.memory` of all scheduled pods plus the new pod exceeds the allocatable capacity of all active worker nodes.
2. **Node Taints and Tolerations:** Worker nodes have taints (e.g., `dedicated=processing:NoSchedule` or `node.kubernetes.io/unreachable`) and the new pod lacks matching `tolerations`.
3. **PersistentVolume (PV) Zone Mismatch:** The pod requires an EBS PersistentVolume located in Availability Zone `us-east-1a`, but all available nodes are in `us-east-1b`.
4. **Exceeded Max Pods per Node / IP Exhaustion:** The AWS VPC CNI on worker nodes has exhausted secondary IP addresses on the node's ENIs, preventing new pods from receiving an IP.
5. **ConfigMap or Secret Missing:** The pod references a ConfigMap or Secret in `envFrom` or `volumeMounts` that does not exist or has a typo in its name.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If the existing nodes lack the resources to schedule new pods, how would you handle this scenario so that new nodes are added and pods get scheduled?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To automatically add new nodes when cluster resources are exhausted:

### Solution: Configure Automated Node Autoscaling
1. **Implement Karpenter (Modern AWS Standard):**
   - Install **Karpenter** in the EKS cluster. Karpenter observes unschedulable pods directly and provisions right-sized EC2 instances in under 45 seconds, perfectly matching the pod's resource requests.
2. **Kubernetes Cluster Autoscaler (Traditional Method):**
   - Deploy the **Cluster Autoscaler** pod linked to the AWS Auto Scaling Group (ASG).
   - When pods enter `Pending` state due to `0/N nodes available: insufficient memory`, Cluster Autoscaler automatically increments the ASG `desired_capacity`, launching fresh EC2 instances.
3. **Overprovisioning / Pause Pods (Buffer Strategy):**
   - Deploy low-priority "pause" pods using Kubernetes PriorityClasses to hold node buffer space that can be evicted instantly by production pods without waiting for new nodes to boot.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● In a blue-green setup using two namespaces in the same Kubernetes cluster, the green environment is brought up only during releases. When it is brought up, the application pod is stuck in ContainerCreating and the events say no PersistentVolume is available, even though the PV and PVC exist with the correct name in the same namespace. What could be the reason, and how would you resolve it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Primary Root Cause:
AWS EBS volumes (`ReadWriteOnce` access mode) **can only be attached to a single EC2 instance in a single Availability Zone at a time**:
1. **Multi-AZ Mismatch:** The existing EBS PV is locked to a specific Availability Zone (e.g., `us-east-1a`). The new green pod was scheduled on a worker node in a different AZ (e.g., `us-east-1b`), so EBS cannot attach across AZ boundaries.
2. **Volume Still Locked by Blue Pod (`Multi-Attach error`):** If both namespaces point to the same underlying EBS volume ID, AWS EBS denies attachment to the green pod because the blue pod is still holding the volume lease.

### How to Resolve It:
- **Solution 1: Use Amazon EFS (Elastic File System) for Shared Storage:**
  Switch the storage class to **EFS (`ReadWriteMany` - RWX)**. EFS supports simultaneous multi-AZ mounting across multiple namespaces without conflict.
- **Solution 2: Node Affinity for EBS PV:**
  Ensure the green pod has `nodeAffinity` matching the PV's exact Availability Zone (`topology.ebs.csi.aws.com/zone: us-east-1a`).
- **Solution 3: Decouple State from Blue-Green Containers:**
  In cloud-native production, applications in blue-green setups should be stateless, storing data in external managed databases (Amazon RDS / DynamoDB / S3) rather than local EBS block volumes.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● If infrastructure is managed by Terraform and someone manually whitelists an IP in the AWS console, causing configuration drift to show up in terraform plan, how would you handle this scenario?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When configuration drift occurs due to an out-of-band manual change:

### Step 1: Align on Intent
Determine whether the manual change was a temporary diagnostic action or an authorized permanent change:
- **If Unauthorized / Temporary:**
  Run `terraform apply`. Terraform will revert the manual change and restore the security group back to the desired state defined in Git.
- **If Legitimate & Permanent:**
  Update the Terraform `.tf` source code in Git to add the whitelisted IP CIDR block. Run `terraform plan` to verify zero diff (`Infrastructure up-to-date`), then commit and merge the PR.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is the purpose of the terraform import command, and why would it be needed to handle drift from a manual change?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Purpose of `terraform import`:
The `terraform import` command brings **existing, unmanaged cloud resources** into the Terraform state file (`terraform.tfstate`) without recreating or deleting them.

### When it is Needed:
- If someone created an entirely **new standalone resource** via the AWS Console (e.g., a brand new Security Group, S3 bucket, or IAM Role), Terraform does not track it.
- To bring it under IaC management:
  1. Write the resource block in `main.tf`: `resource "aws_security_group" "new_sg" {}`.
  2. Run `terraform import aws_security_group.new_sg sg-0123456789abcdef`.
  3. Run `terraform plan` and align attributes until zero diff is achieved.

*(Note: If someone simply modified a rule inside an existing managed Security Group, `terraform import` is not needed; `terraform refresh` automatically detects the drift in attributes).*
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● You add a new security group rule to existing Terraform code, and terraform fmt and validate succeed, but the new rule does not appear in the terraform plan output, even though the same code is used by the pipeline. What could be the reason?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Common reasons why a validated Terraform change does not show up in `terraform plan`:

1. **Uncommitted / Unpushed Code:** The changes were saved locally but not committed or pushed to the branch evaluated by the CI/CD pipeline runner.
2. **Targeting the Wrong Directory or Workspace:** The pipeline executed `terraform plan` inside a different environment directory (e.g., ran in `environments/dev` while you edited `environments/prod`), or a different Terraform Workspace.
3. **Module Not Re-Initialized (`terraform get` / `terraform init`):** If the rule was added inside a child module, the root module uses the cached module in `.terraform/modules/`. Run `terraform get -update` or `terraform init -upgrade` to pull the module changes.
4. **Conditional Logic (`count = 0` or `false` flag):** The rule or resource contains a condition (e.g., `count = var.enable_rule ? 1 : 0`) and the variable in `.tfvars` evaluates to false.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a module in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **module** in Terraform is a container for multiple resources that are used together. In fact, any directory containing a set of `.tf` files is a module.

- **Root Module:** The working directory where you execute `terraform init` and `terraform apply`.
- **Child Module:** A module that is called from another module using a `module` block to encapsulate and reuse infrastructure logic (e.g., a VPC module called across Dev, QA, and Prod).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you call a child module from a root module in Terraform?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You call a child module using a **`module` block** specifying the `source` path and required input variables:

```hcl
module "vpc" {
  source = "./modules/vpc"    # Local path or remote Git URL

  # Passing Input Variables
  vpc_cidr           = "10.0.0.0/16"
  public_subnet_cidrs = ["10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"]
  environment        = "production"
}
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does the parent (root) module call a child module in Terraform and pass values to it, and what happens internally between the parent and child modules?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### How Values Pass Between Parent & Child:
1. **Inputs:** The parent module defines argument key-values in the `module` block. The child module declares those expected inputs in its `variables.tf`.
2. **Outputs:** The child module computes resources and exports values using `output` blocks in `outputs.tf`.
3. **Parent Consuming Child Outputs:** The parent accesses the child's outputs using the syntax `module.<module_name>.<output_name>`.

### What Happens Internally:
- During `terraform init`, Terraform copies child modules into the hidden `.terraform/modules/` directory.
- During `terraform plan`, Terraform builds a single unified **Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG)** of all resources across root and child modules, automatically calculating dependencies to determine creation order.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you use Terraform to create four EC2 servers where two have identical configurations and two have different configurations?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The cleanest and most maintainable way is using **`for_each`** over a map of objects in Terraform:

```hcl
variable "servers" {
  type = map(object({
    instance_type = string
    name          = string
  }))
  default = {
    # Two identical web servers
    "web-01" = { instance_type = "t3.micro", name = "web-prod-01" }
    "web-02" = { instance_type = "t3.micro", name = "web-prod-02" }
    
    # Two differently configured servers
    "app-01" = { instance_type = "t3.small", name = "backend-api" }
    "db-01"  = { instance_type = "m5.large", name = "database-cache" }
  }
}

resource "aws_instance" "server" {
  for_each      = var.servers
  ami           = "ami-0c55b159cbfafe1f0"
  instance_type = each.value.instance_type

  tags = {
    Name = each.value.name
  }
}
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does the for_each meta-argument work in Terraform when creating multiple resources?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The **`for_each`** meta-argument iterates over a map or a set of strings, creating an independent resource instance for each item.

### Key Working Principles:
1. **Map Key Identification:** Each resource is identified in state by its map key rather than an integer index:
   `aws_instance.server["web-01"]` instead of `aws_instance.server[0]`.
2. **`each.key` and `each.value`:** Inside the resource block, you access the current key via `each.key` and attributes via `each.value`.
3. **Safe Additions and Removals:** Unlike `count`, deleting an item from the middle of the collection will **only destroy that specific resource**, without causing Terraform to recreate subsequent resources due to shifting array indices.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Terraform state file is accidentally removed, what would you do to recover?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If the Terraform state file (`terraform.tfstate`) is lost:

1. **Restore from S3 Bucket Versioning (Immediate Solution):**
   - In production, remote state S3 buckets must have **Object Versioning enabled**.
   - Navigate to the S3 bucket, locate the deleted state file, and delete the latest "Delete Marker" or restore the previous version.
2. **Restore from S3 Cross-Region Replication / Backup:**
   - Restore from the backup bucket or AWS Backup vault.
3. **Rebuild State via `terraform import` (Disaster Recovery Fallback):**
   - If no backup exists, you must inspect the existing AWS infrastructure, re-write the matching resource blocks, and run `terraform import <resource> <id>` for each resource to reconstruct the state file without recreating resources.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Where do you store your Terraform state file in your organization?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our organization, we store Terraform state remotely in an **Amazon S3 bucket** with **DynamoDB state locking** configured via the standard S3 backend:

```hcl
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "myorg-terraform-state-prod"
    key            = "eks-cluster/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-lock-table"
    encrypt        = true
  }
}
```

### Security & Reliability Safeguards:
- **AES-256 Encryption:** Encrypted at rest using AWS KMS.
- **S3 Bucket Versioning:** Prevents accidental deletion and preserves full audit history.
- **DynamoDB State Locking:** Prevents concurrent `terraform apply` executions from corrupting the state file.
- **Restricted IAM Access:** Strict S3 bucket policies allowing only approved CI/CD pipeline roles to read/write state files.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If the Terraform state file stored in an S3 bucket is accidentally deleted by someone, how would you recover it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Step-by-Step S3 Versioning Recovery:

1. **Enable "Show Versions" in S3:**
   - In AWS Console, open the state bucket and toggle on **Show versions** (or use AWS CLI).
2. **Locate the Delete Marker:**
   - S3 does not physically destroy versioned objects on deletion; it places a **Delete Marker** as the current version.
3. **Delete the Delete Marker:**
   - Execute AWS CLI command:
     ```bash
     aws s3api delete-object --bucket myorg-terraform-state-prod        --key eks-cluster/terraform.tfstate        --version-id <DeleteMarkerVersionId>
     ```
   - Removing the delete marker immediately restores the previous valid state file as the active version.
4. **Verify Recovery:**
   - Run `terraform plan` locally or in the pipeline. It will connect to the restored state and display the correct current infrastructure without attempting to recreate existing resources.
</details>

</details>

</details>
