#### 【 GIT 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Why and when do you use git stash in Git?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`git stash` temporarily shelves (or stashes) uncommitted changes (both staged and unstaged modifications in the working directory) without committing them to the Git history, reverting your working tree back to the clean `HEAD` commit state.

---

### When to Use `git stash`:
1. **Urgent Hotfix or Context Switching:** You are midway through developing a feature on branch `feature-xyz` with incomplete, non-compiling code, and an urgent production bug requires you to switch immediately to `main` or `hotfix`.
2. **Pulling Upstream Changes Cleanly:** When running `git pull origin main` would fail due to local merge conflicts with uncommitted changes. Stashing local changes allows pulling smoothly, after which changes can be reapplied:
   ```bash
   git stash
   git pull origin main --rebase
   git stash pop
   ```

---

### Key Commands:
- `git stash push -m "work-in-progress on risk engine"`: Stashes with a descriptive message.
- `git stash push -u`: Stashes including **untracked** files.
- `git stash list`: Lists all stashed snapshots (`stash@{0}`, `stash@{1}`).
- `git stash pop`: Restores the most recently stashed changes and removes them from the stash list.
- `git stash apply stash@{1}`: Applies a specific stash without dropping it from the list.
- `git stash drop stash@{0}` / `git stash clear`: Removes specific or all stashed entries.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is git rebase and when would you use it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
`git rebase` is the process of moving or reapplying a sequence of commits from one branch on top of another base tip, creating a **clean, linear commit history** without cluttering the repository with unnecessary merge commits.

---

### How Rebase Works:
When you execute `git rebase main` on a feature branch:
1. Git identifies the common ancestor commit of `feature` and `main`.
2. Git temporarily sets aside all commits made on the `feature` branch.
3. Git fast-forwards the `feature` branch to point to the current tip of `main`.
4. Git reapplies the set-aside commits one-by-one onto the new tip of `main`.

---

### When to Use `git rebase`:
1. **Keeping Feature Branches Up-to-Date:** Before opening a Pull Request, rebasing your feature branch against `origin/main` incorporates all new team changes cleanly.
2. **Maintaining a Clean Linear History:** Avoids messy "Merge branch 'main' into feature" commits that complicate `git log` and `git bisect` debugging.

---

### The Golden Rule of Rebasing:
**Never rebase a shared public branch** (like `main` or `staging`) that other developers are actively branching from! Rebasing rewrites commit SHA hashes; if executed on public branches, it will force all collaborators to resolve massive divergence.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: In the context of git rebase, what does squashing commits mean and how is it done?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Squashing commits** means combining multiple intermediate, messy, or work-in-progress commits (e.g., "wip", "fixed typo", "more tests") into a **single, meaningful, cohesive commit** before merging into the main branch.

---

### How to Squash Commits via Interactive Rebase:
To squash the last 4 commits on your feature branch:
```bash
git rebase -i HEAD~4
```

Git opens an editor displaying the last 4 commits in chronological order:
```text
pick 4a1b2c3 Add seller fraud velocity rule
pick 9f8e7d6 Fix syntax typo in redis key
pick 2b3c4d5 Add unit test for velocity rule
pick 7e6d5c4 Update README comments

# Commands:
# p, pick = use commit
# s, squash = use commit, but meld into previous commit
```

1. Keep the first commit as `pick` and change the subsequent commits to `squash` (or `s`):
   ```text
   pick 4a1b2c3 Add seller fraud velocity rule
   s 9f8e7d6 Fix syntax typo in redis key
   s 2b3c4d5 Add unit test for velocity rule
   s 7e6d5c4 Update README comments
   ```
2. Save and exit the editor.
3. Git prompts for a single consolidated commit message. Enter a clean commit message adhering to Conventional Commits:
   ```text
   feat(risk-engine): implement seller velocity detection rule and unit tests
   ```
4. Push the cleaned history to remote using force with lease:
   ```bash
   git push --force-with-lease origin feature-velocity-rule
   ```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What Git branching strategy have you used in your projects?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our enterprise microservices projects, we follow **GitHub Flow (Short-Lived Feature Branching)**, augmented with release tags for production:

---

### Strategy Breakdown:
1. **`main` Branch (Production-Ready):**
   - Protected branch. Direct pushes are disabled.
   - Requires pull request reviews (minimum 2 approvals), automated CI status checks passing (unit tests, SonarQube, Trivy), and branch to be up to date before merging.
2. **Short-Lived Feature Branches (`feature/JIRA-123-seller-kyc`):**
   - Branched directly from latest `main`.
   - Lifespan is short (typically 1 to 3 days) to minimize merge drift.
3. **Bugfix & Hotfix Branches (`hotfix/JIRA-999-albc-timeout`):**
   - Cut directly from `main` or production release tag for emergency patches, merged back to `main` immediately upon approval.
4. **Semantic Release Tags (`v2.4.0`):**
   - Merging to `main` automatically triggers deployment to Staging. Once verified, creating a Git release tag (e.g., `v2.4.0`) triggers promotion to Production.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is the branching, PR, and deployment workflow you described completely automated?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes, the CI validation and lower-environment deployment workflow is 100% automated via webhooks and pipelines:**

- **On PR Creation / Update:** GitHub Webhook instantly triggers a Jenkins PR verification pipeline that checks out the branch, executes unit tests, runs SonarQube static analysis, and builds a test container. The PR is blocked from merging if any automated check fails.
- **On PR Merge to `main`:** Automatically triggers the CD pipeline to deploy to the **DEV** and **STAGING** environments without human intervention.
- **Production Gate:** The promotion to **PROD** is automated end-to-end (Helm rollout, canary routing, health check validation), but requires an explicit **manual approval click** by an authorized release lead in Jenkins or via ChatOps in Slack.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Does every code change automatically get deployed through your pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **In DEV and STAGING:** **Yes**, every merged code change is deployed automatically to ensure rapid feedback and automated integration testing.
- **In PRODUCTION:** **No.** While the pipeline execution itself is fully automated, deployment to production requires passing strict governance criteria:
  1. Successful automated smoke tests in Staging.
  2. Scheduled deployment window compliance (off-peak hours).
  3. Formal Change Advisory Board (CAB) or Tech Lead digital approval.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What do you use for deployments, and what is the target environment where the applications are deployed?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Deployment Tooling:** We use **Helm charts** packaged and triggered via our declarative **Jenkins pipelines** (and Argo CD in GitOps clusters).
- **Target Environments:**
  1. **DEV Environment:** Amazon EKS cluster in the non-production AWS account (`dev` namespace).
  2. **STAGING / UAT Environment:** Mirror of production in a staging AWS account for integration testing and load tests.
  3. **PRODUCTION Environment:** High-availability Amazon EKS cluster running across 3 Availability Zones in our dedicated production AWS account.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you generate the Docker images for your applications?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We generate Docker images using **multi-stage Docker builds** automated inside our CI pipeline:

---

### Step-by-Step Image Generation:
1. **Base Image Selection:** We start from hardened, verified official base images (e.g., `eclipse-temurin:17-jdk-jammy` for building and `eclipse-temurin:17-jre-jammy` or Google `distroless` for runtime).
2. **Multi-Stage Build Pipeline:**
   - **Stage 1 (Builder):** Compiles code, downloads dependencies (`maven`, `go build`, `npm install`), and generates production binaries.
   - **Stage 2 (Runtime):** Copies *only* the compiled binary into a minimal runtime image. Build tools, package managers, and source code are discarded.
3. **Security Hardening:**
   - Create a dedicated non-root user and group (`USER appuser:appgroup`).
   - Eliminate unnecessary packages, shell binaries, and compilers.
4. **Immutable Tagging & Registry Push:**
   - Tagged with the Git commit SHA: `myorg/seller-risk-svc:git-3f8a91c`.
   - Pushed securely to **Amazon ECR** over authenticated AWS IAM OIDC sessions.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: At what point in the workflow (for example, at which stage or event) is the Docker image built?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The Docker image is built **during the CI phase, immediately after unit tests and static code analysis (SonarQube) pass successfully**:

```text
[Checkout Code] ──> [Unit Tests] ──> [SonarQube Gate] ──> [Docker Build] ──> [Trivy Scan] ──> [Push to ECR]
```

It is triggered on:
- Merging a feature PR into `main` (for release deployment).
- Creating a release tag.
Building the image *after* unit tests ensures that we never waste runner compute packaging code that fails basic test suites.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How many times do you build the same Docker image across your CI and CD pipelines?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We build the Docker image **EXACTLY ONCE**.

---

### The "Build Once, Deploy Everywhere" Principle:
In production-grade DevOps, rebuilding the same application code across different pipeline stages or environments is an anti-pattern. Building an image generates a unique cryptographic SHA256 image digest.

By building **once** in the CI pipeline:
1. That single image is tagged with the unique Git commit SHA (`git-3a7b1c8`).
2. It is scanned by security scanners (Trivy).
3. The exact same image artifact is promoted and deployed sequentially across Dev, QA, Staging, and Production.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Why do you build the Docker image twice, once in the CI pipeline and once in the CD pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
*(Clarifying the interviewer's premise)*:
If a pipeline is building the Docker image twice—once in CI and once in CD—that represents a **flawed architecture that should be immediately corrected**.

---

### Why Building Twice is an Anti-Pattern:
1. **Loss of Immutability:** A Docker image built at 10:00 AM in CI and rebuilt at 2:00 PM in CD may download newer base image patches or dependency updates, resulting in different runtime behavior.
2. **Invalidated Security Audits:** The Trivy or Snyk scan performed in CI is completely invalidated because the image digest deployed in CD is not the one that was scanned!
3. **Wasted Compute & Time:** Compiling and packaging microservices twice adds 5-15 minutes of unnecessary pipeline delay.

---

### How We Do It Properly:
In our setup, the **CI pipeline builds, scans, and pushes the image**. The **CD pipeline does NOT build an image**; it simply consumes the image tag and deploys it to the target Kubernetes cluster via Helm.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Are you saying the Docker image is built separately for each branch and environment (dev, staging, and main)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No.** We do **NOT** build separate images for each environment. We follow **Twelve-Factor App methodology**: **Code and configuration are strictly separated.**

---

### How Single-Image Multi-Environment Promotion Works:
1. **One Universal Image:** The Docker image contains strictly the compiled application code and runtime dependencies—it contains **zero environment-specific configurations, passwords, or endpoints**.
2. **Externalized Configuration:** Environment differences (e.g., whether to connect to `db-dev.internal` or `db-prod.internal`) are injected at runtime via Kubernetes **ConfigMaps** and **Secrets** (via AWS Secrets Manager).
3. **Artifact Promotion Flow:**
   - Code merged to `main` -> Build image `app:git-a1b2c3d`.
   - Deploy `app:git-a1b2c3d` to **DEV** (using `values-dev.yaml`).
   - Once verified, deploy the **exact same** `app:git-a1b2c3d` to **STAGING** (using `values-staging.yaml`).
   - Upon final sign-off, deploy that **exact same** `app:git-a1b2c3d` to **PROD** (using `values-prod.yaml`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If images are built for every branch, which one do you actually use for deployment, and aren't they all the same image?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Feature Branch Images (Ephemeral / Testing Only):**
   - When a developer opens a PR from `feature-x`, CI can build a temporary image tagged `feature-x-<commit>` purely for automated integration tests in an isolated ephemeral namespace.
   - These images are **never deployed to Production**.
2. **Main Branch Images (Release Artifacts):**
   - Only images built from the canonical **`main` branch** (or official Git release tags) are designated as **Release Candidates**.
   - These images are pushed to Amazon ECR with immutable tags (`git-<SHA>`).
3. **Which One is Used for Deployment?**
   - Only the image generated from the merged `main` commit is promoted through Dev -> Staging -> Prod. Images built from dirty feature branches are discarded or auto-deleted via ECR lifecycle policies after 7 days.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Isn't building the same image multiple times redundant? Why would you waste resources repeating the same build?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**It is completely redundant and wasteful.** That is precisely why modern DevOps architectures strictly mandate **"Build Once, Deploy Many."**

By eliminating duplicate builds:
- We save **hundreds of build minutes** per day across 25 microservices.
- We eliminate cloud runner compute costs.
- We achieve **100% deterministic deployments**, guaranteeing that the binary running in production is bit-for-bit identical to the binary that passed QA automated regression testing.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which Docker image is used in which environment, and how do you differentiate and isolate the images between environments such as dev, staging, and production?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We differentiate environments through **deployment manifests and runtime configuration, NOT by changing the Docker image itself**:

---

### How Environments are Isolated:
1. **The Shared Binary:** All three environments (Dev, Staging, Prod) run the exact same container image: `123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/risk-engine:git-3f8a91c`.
2. **Environment Separation Mechanisms:**
   - **Isolated AWS Accounts / Clusters:** Dev runs in the Dev AWS Account; Prod runs in the Prod AWS Account.
   - **Environment Values Files:**
     - `values-dev.yaml`: Sets `replicas: 2`, `cpu_request: 250m`, logs at `DEBUG`.
     - `values-prod.yaml`: Sets `replicas: 10`, `cpu_request: 2000m`, multi-AZ anti-affinity, logs at `INFO`.
   - **Dynamic Secret Injection:** In Dev, the pod pulls credentials from `arn:aws:secretsmanager:...:secret:dev/db`. In Prod, the pod pulls from `arn:aws:secretsmanager:...:secret:prod/db` via IRSA.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: So are you saying that the images used in each environment are all different images?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No, they are the EXACT SAME image.**

To state it clearly:
- **Image:** 100% identical binary across Dev, Staging, and Production.
- **Configuration & Secrets:** 100% isolated and environment-specific, injected dynamically from outside the container at runtime.

If an image had to be different for each environment, you would be re-introducing untested changes into production, breaking continuous delivery.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What stages did you set up in your CI/CD pipelines for builds and deployments?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Our standardized declarative pipeline consists of 8 automated stages:

1. **`Checkout & Validation`:** Pulls code using shallow clone and validates branch naming and commit rules.
2. **`Unit Test & Code Coverage`:** Runs unit tests and verifies code coverage with JaCoCo / Go test tools.
3. **`SonarQube SAST Analysis`:** Analyzes code quality, technical debt, and enforces the Quality Gate.
4. **`Docker Build`:** Executes multi-stage build producing an optimized runtime image.
5. **`Trivy Vulnerability Scan`:** Scans container image for OS and package CVEs.
6. **`Push to Registry`:** Pushes image with Git SHA tag to Amazon ECR.
7. **`Deploy to Dev / Staging`:** Updates Helm release in the target EKS cluster and verifies rollout.
8. **`Automated Smoke Tests & Notifications`:** Executes synthetic curl checks and posts rich notifications to Slack.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Are you sure the CD pipeline also creates the Docker image again, rather than reusing the image built in the CI pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**I am 100% certain that our CD pipeline DOES NOT create the Docker image again.** It **reuses** the image built during CI.

The CD stage takes the image tag generated in CI (e.g., `git-8b455c4`) as a pipeline parameter and executes:
```bash
helm upgrade --install seller-risk-svc ./charts/seller-risk-svc   --set image.tag="git-8b455c4"   --namespace production
```
Kubernetes then pulls the pre-existing, pre-scanned image from Amazon ECR.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does your CD deployment pipeline create the image and perform the deployment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
As emphasized, the CD deployment pipeline **does not create the image**. It performs the deployment through the following automated steps:

1. **Receive Image Tag:** Receives the immutable image tag (`git-commit-sha`) from the upstream CI pipeline or Git release tag.
2. **AWS Authentication:** Assumes the CD deployment IAM role via AWS OIDC (no hardcoded keys).
3. **Kubeconfig Context:** Retrieves cluster credentials:
   ```bash
   aws eks update-kubeconfig --region us-east-1 --name prod-eks-cluster
   ```
4. **Helm Upgrade Rollout:**
   ```bash
   helm upgrade --install seller-risk-service ./helm/seller-risk      --namespace production      --values ./helm/seller-risk/values-prod.yaml      --set image.tag="${IMAGE_TAG}"      --wait --timeout 5m
   ```
5. **Rollout Verification:** Watches `kubectl rollout status deployment/seller-risk-service -n production`. If pods fail readiness probes, Helm automatically initiates a rollback to the previous revision.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a multi-stage Docker build and why is it used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **multi-stage Docker build** is a method of authoring Dockerfiles using multiple `FROM` instructions, allowing you to use different base images for different stages of the build process and selectively copy artifacts from one stage to another.

---

### Why It Is Used:
1. **Dramatically Smaller Image Size:** Eliminates build-time dependencies, SDKs, compilers (JDK, Maven, Go toolchain), reducing image size from ~1 GB down to ~80 MB.
2. **Enhanced Security (Reduced Attack Surface):** Vulnerabilities in build tools or package managers (curl, git, compilers) are never present in the final production runtime container.
3. **No Intermediate Layer Leaks:** Secrets or tokens used to download private dependencies during compilation are discarded with the builder stage.

---

### Example Multi-Stage Dockerfile (Java Spring Boot):
```dockerfile
# Stage 1: Build & Package
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-17 AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn dependency:go-offline
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn clean package -DskipTests

# Stage 2: Minimal Production Runtime
FROM eclipse-temurin:17-jre-jammy AS runner
WORKDIR /app
# Create non-root user
RUN addgroup --system appgroup && adduser --system appuser --ingroup appgroup
USER appuser
# Copy ONLY the compiled jar from builder stage
COPY --from=builder /app/target/seller-risk-service-*.jar app.jar
EXPOSE 8080
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]
```
</details>

#### 【 SECURITY 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you ensure that your systems and your complete organization's infrastructure are secure?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We implement a **Defense-in-Depth (Multi-Layered Security)** architecture spanning the entire software development and cloud operational lifecycle:

1. **Shift-Left Application Security:**
   - **Secret Detection:** Git hooks with `gitleaks` prevent developers from committing credentials.
   - **SAST:** SonarQube quality gates run in CI to catch OWASP Top 10 vulnerabilities.
   - **SCA & Container Scanning:** Trivy scans dependencies and container images before pushing to Amazon ECR.
2. **Infrastructure as Code (IaC) Security:**
   - `checkov` and `tfsec` scan Terraform modules for misconfigurations (e.g., unencrypted S3 buckets, open security group ingress `0.0.0.0/0`).
3. **Cloud & Network Perimeter Security:**
   - Multi-AZ VPC with private subnets for all workloads.
   - AWS WAF with rate-limiting and SQLi/XSS inspection rules attached to internet-facing ALBs.
   - Restrict administrative access via AWS Systems Manager Session Manager (no open port 22).
4. **Identity & Access Management:**
   - Least privilege RBAC, IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA), mandatory MFA, and temporary STS credentials.
5. **Operating System & Runtime Hardening:**
   - Minimal base images, read-only root filesystems where applicable, non-root container users, and automated AMI patching.
6. **Continuous Compliance & Threat Detection:**
   - AWS GuardDuty, AWS Security Hub, and CloudTrail enabled across all AWS accounts.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is OS hardening?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**OS Hardening** is the practice of securing an operating system by eliminating security vulnerabilities and reducing its attack surface to the bare minimum required to execute its intended function:

---

### Core OS Hardening Techniques:
1. **Remove Unnecessary Packages & Services:**
   - Uninstall unused daemons (FTP, Telnet, rsh, postfix) and compilers from production servers.
2. **User & Authentication Hardening:**
   - Disable the `root` SSH login (`PermitRootLogin no` in `/etc/ssh/sshd_config`).
   - Disable password-based SSH authentication (`PasswordAuthentication no`); require SSH keys or SSM Session Manager.
   - Enforce strong password complexity and account lockouts after failed login attempts using PAM.
3. **Firewall & Network Configuration:**
   - Enable host-level firewall (`ufw` or `iptables`) blocking all inbound ports except application ports.
   - Disable ICMP redirect acceptance and IP forwarding in sysctl (`/etc/sysctl.conf`).
4. **Filesystem Permissions & Audit:**
   - Apply CIS Benchmarks (Center for Internet Security).
   - Enforce strict permissions (`chmod 600 /etc/shadow`, `chmod 644 /etc/passwd`).
   - Configure Linux audit daemon (`auditd`) to monitor privilege escalation commands.
5. **Kernel Security Modules:**
   - Enable SELinux (in Enforcing mode) or AppArmor to confine container processes.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What does a Trivy scan give you, and what does it check for in your pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Trivy** (by Aqua Security) is a comprehensive, high-speed open-source security scanner. In our CI/CD pipeline, it provides a detailed vulnerability report checking for:

1. **OS Package Vulnerabilities:** Scans operating system packages installed in the container (e.g., vulnerabilities in `openssl`, `glibc`, `curl`, `busybox` against Debian/Alpine/Ubuntu security databases).
2. **Application Dependencies (SCA):** Scans language package manager lockfiles (`pom.xml`, `package-lock.json`, `go.sum`, `requirements.txt`) for known Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs).
3. **Misconfigurations:** Scans Dockerfiles and Kubernetes manifests for security misconfigurations (e.g., running as root, missing resource limits, privileged flags).
4. **Secret Leaks:** Detects exposed API keys, passwords, and tokens inadvertently baked into container layers.

---

### Pipeline Output:
Trivy outputs structured CVE IDs (e.g., `CVE-2024-1234`), affected package name, installed version, fixed version, severity level (`UNKNOWN`, `LOW`, `MEDIUM`, `HIGH`, `CRITICAL`), and a brief description.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What do you do about the issues or vulnerabilities reported by the Trivy scan?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
When Trivy reports vulnerabilities, we follow a prioritized remediation process based on **severity and exploitability**:

1. **Filter by Severity & Fix Availability:**
   - In the pipeline, we configure Trivy with `--ignore-unfixed` and `--severity HIGH,CRITICAL` to focus on actionable risks with available patches.
2. **Triage the Report:**
   - Check if a fixed version is published (`Fixed Version` column in Trivy report).
3. **Remediate:**
   - **OS-Level CVE:** Update the base image in the Dockerfile (e.g., from `node:18.16.0` to `node:18.20.0-alpine`).
   - **Application Library CVE:** Upgrade the dependency version in `pom.xml` or `package.json`.
4. **False Positive / Mitigation Whitelisting:**
   - If a CVE has no fix available or is non-exploitable in our architecture, it can be documented and temporarily suppressed using a `.trivyignore` file with an approved JIRA security exception ticket.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: When the Trivy scan finds vulnerabilities, what action do you take?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Immediate Pipeline Gate:**
   - If Trivy detects any `CRITICAL` or `HIGH` vulnerability that has an available fix, the pipeline command exits with code 1 (`--exit-code 1`), **immediately failing the CI build** and blocking container image promotion to Amazon ECR.
2. **Automated Notification:**
   - The pipeline triggers `slackNotify.groovy`, sending an alert to the `#devops-security-alerts` and team channels with the build URL, developer author, and CVE summary.
3. **Ticket Creation & Escalation:**
   - High-severity findings automatically generate a Jira bug ticket assigned to the responsible engineering squad.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: After the CI pipeline fails because of the Trivy scan findings, what happens next?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
1. **Build Quarantine:** The Docker image is **not** pushed to the production container registry, and the pull request cannot be merged.
2. **Developer / DevOps Triage:**
   - The developer or DevOps engineer inspects the Jenkins build console or Trivy HTML artifact to identify whether the issue is an **OS package** or an **application library**.
3. **Patching & Re-testing:**
   - The responsible engineer bumps the library version or base image in a new commit pushed to the feature branch.
4. **Automated Re-scan:**
   - Pushing the fix re-triggers the CI pipeline. Once Trivy confirms zero critical vulnerabilities, the quality gate turns green and the PR becomes eligible for merge.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: When the Trivy scan fails, is fixing the issues only the developer's responsibility, or do you also have to make changes?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
It is a **shared responsibility model** between DevOps and Developers depending on the layer where the vulnerability resides:

---

### Division of Responsibility:
| Vulnerability Layer | Primary Responsibility | Action Taken |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Application Dependencies** (e.g., vulnerable Log4j, Jackson, or npm packages) | **Developer Squad** | The developer updates `pom.xml` or `package.json`, runs unit/integration tests to ensure no breaking API changes, and commits the fix. |
| **Base OS / Docker Infrastructure** (e.g., outdated Debian packages, Dockerfile syntax, running as root) | **DevOps / Platform Engineer** | The DevOps engineer updates the base Dockerfile, re-evaluates golden base images, updates pipeline scanner definitions, or adds runtime security profiles. |
| **Zero-Day with No Fix Available** | **Joint Effort** | DevOps and Dev collaborate with the security team to implement compensating controls (e.g., adding an AWS WAF rule or blocking specific request payloads) and document a `.trivyignore` exception. |
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How does a Docker container work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Docker container is **not a virtual machine**. It does not run its own guest operating system or hypervisor. Instead, a container is simply **an isolated Linux process running directly on the host kernel**, isolated through two fundamental Linux kernel features:

---

### Core Linux Kernel Mechanisms:
1. **Namespaces (Isolation - What the process can see):**
   - `PID Namespace`: Isolates process IDs (the container process appears as PID 1 inside the container, even though it has a regular PID on the host).
   - `NET Namespace`: Provides isolated network interfaces, IP addresses, and routing tables.
   - `MNT (Mount) Namespace`: Provides an isolated filesystem mount hierarchy.
   - `IPC / UTS / User Namespaces`: Isolate inter-process communication, hostnames, and user mappings.
2. **Control Groups (cgroups - What the process can use):**
   - Governs resource metering and limits. Sets maximum caps on CPU, memory, disk I/O, and network bandwidth, preventing a container from starving the host.
3. **OverlayFS (Union File System):**
   - Stacks immutable read-only image layers together with a thin read-write container layer on top for copy-on-write file modifications.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between CMD and ENTRYPOINT in a Dockerfile?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Both instructions define the default command executed when a container starts, but they differ in how they handle command-line arguments:

---

### Key Differences:
| Instruction | Purpose | Override Behavior |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **`ENTRYPOINT`** | Defines the **core, fixed executable** that the container is intended to run as a binary. | Difficult to override from CLI; requires explicit `--entrypoint <new-binary>`. |
| **`CMD`** | Defines **default arguments** for the `ENTRYPOINT`, or default standalone command. | **Easily overridden** by appending arguments at the end of `docker run`. |

---

### Best Practice: Combining Both (Exec Form):
```dockerfile
# Exec form (recommended JSON array syntax)
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "/app/app.jar"]
CMD ["--server.port=8080"]
```
- Running `docker run my-app`:
  Executes `java -jar /app/app.jar --server.port=8080`.
- Running `docker run my-app --server.port=9090`:
  Overrides `CMD` easily, executing `java -jar /app/app.jar --server.port=9090`.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● If a Docker container starts without any error and then suddenly stops, what could be the reason?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A Docker container remains running **only as long as its foreground primary process (PID 1) remains alive**. If a container starts and immediately stops, the most common root causes are:

---

### Root Causes & Investigation:
1. **Foreground Process Finished (Exit Code 0):**
   - The container entrypoint was a script or command that completed its execution (e.g., `echo "Hello World"` or `systemctl start nginx` which exits immediately after launching a background daemon).
2. **Application Crashed on Startup:**
   - Unhandled exception during startup (missing database connection, invalid environment variables, file permission denied).
3. **Out-of-Memory (OOMKilled - Exit Code 137):**
   - The application tried to allocate more RAM than allowed by the container memory limit.

---

### How to Check:
```bash
# Check container exit code and termination time:
docker ps -a --filter "status=exited"

# View logs from the terminated container:
docker logs <container-id>

# Inspect detailed state:
docker inspect <container-id> --format='{{.State.ExitCode}} {{.State.OOMKilled}} {{.State.Error}}'
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What if the Docker container stops gracefully without showing any errors? What could be the cause?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If the container stops gracefully with **Exit Code 0** and no error logs, it means the primary process (PID 1) completed its task and terminated normally.

---

### Common Causes:
1. **Background Daemon Misconfiguration:**
   - Example: Running `service nginx start` or `nginx` without `daemon off;`. The script forks Nginx into the background and the startup script exits cleanly. Because PID 1 exited, Docker terminates the container!
2. **Interactive Shell with No TTY:**
   - Example: Running `CMD ["/bin/bash"]` without passing `-it` flags to `docker run`. Since stdin closed immediately, bash saw EOF and exited gracefully.
3. **Completed Batch / Job Script:**
   - The container was packaged to run a one-time migration or cleanup script (`python migrate.py`) that successfully executed all statements and reached the end of the script.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Why does a Docker container exit gracefully after its process finishes, and when does this happen?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Docker's design philosophy is centered on **process isolation**. The lifecycle of a container is directly tied to its **PID 1 (the entrypoint process)**:

---

### The Operating System Mechanics:
- When Docker creates a container, it launches the specified `ENTRYPOINT` / `CMD` as PID 1 in the container's PID namespace.
- In Linux, when process PID 1 exits (terminates with exit code 0 or any signal), the kernel automatically sends `SIGKILL` to all remaining child processes in that PID namespace and tears down the namespace.
- This happens whenever:
  - A shell script reaches its final line.
  - A Python or Go program finishes executing `main()`.
  - A CLI tool finishes printing output.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What is required for a Docker container to keep running continuously?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
For a Docker container to stay running continuously as a daemon, **its primary entrypoint process (PID 1) must run continuously in the FOREGROUND and must not terminate or fork into the background**:

---

### Examples of Keeping Containers Running:
1. **Web Servers / Daemons in Foreground Mode:**
   - Nginx: `CMD ["nginx", "-g", "daemon off;"]`
   - Apache: `CMD ["httpd", "-D", "FOREGROUND"]`
   - Java Spring Boot: `ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]` (runs web server listening on port in foreground).
2. **Worker / Queue Consumers:**
   - Continuous event loop: `python worker.py` (running `while True:` polling Kafka or SQS).
3. **Debug / Base Containers:**
   - If keeping a debugging container alive:
     ```bash
     docker run -d ubuntu sleep infinity
     # Or:
     tail -f /dev/null
     ```
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is an AMI (Amazon Machine Image) in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An **AMI (Amazon Machine Image)** is a pre-configured template used to launch Amazon EC2 virtual machines in AWS.

---

### Key Components of an AMI:
1. **Root Volume Template:** A snapshot of an Amazon EBS volume containing the operating system, installed software packages, libraries, and application code.
2. **Launch Permissions:** Governs which AWS accounts are authorized to use the AMI (Public, Private, or Shared with specific AWS Account IDs).
3. **Block Device Mapping:** Specifies which EBS volumes or instance store volumes attach to the instance upon launching.

---

### Golden AMI Concept:
In enterprise DevOps, we build **Golden AMIs** (using tools like HashiCorp Packer) pre-baked with OS security patches, monitoring agents (CloudWatch/Datadog agent), and CIS security baselines, ensuring fast, deterministic autoscaling without running lengthy setup scripts on boot.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What if you want to create an EC2 instance from an AMI in a different AWS region?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AMIs are **strictly regional resources**. An AMI created in `us-east-1` (N. Virginia) cannot be launched directly in `us-west-2` (Oregon) or `eu-west-1` (Ireland).

---

### Solution:
You must **copy the AMI to the target region** first using the AWS Management Console, AWS CLI, or Terraform:
```bash
aws ec2 copy-image   --source-region us-east-1   --source-image-id ami-0123456789abcdef0   --name "golden-ami-us-west-2"   --region us-west-2
```
Once the copy completes in `us-west-2` and receives a new local `ami-xxxx` identifier, you can launch EC2 instances from it in that region.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you create an EBS volume from an EBS snapshot?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
You create an EBS volume from a snapshot using the AWS Console, AWS CLI, or Terraform:

---

### Using AWS CLI:
```bash
aws ec2 create-volume   --snapshot-id snap-0123456789abcdef0   --availability-zone us-east-1a   --volume-type gp3   --region us-east-1
```

---

### Key Considerations:
1. **Availability Zone (AZ):** An EBS volume is strictly tied to a single AZ. You must specify the exact AZ (e.g., `us-east-1a`) where the target EC2 instance resides.
2. **Volume Size:** The new volume size can be **equal to or larger** than the snapshot size, but cannot be smaller.
3. **Volume Type:** You can change volume types during creation (e.g., restoring an older `gp2` snapshot into a modern, cheaper `gp3` volume with custom IOPS).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you have an AMI in one region and try to launch an instance from it in another region, will it work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No, it will not work.** The AWS API or CLI will return an `InvalidAMIID.NotFound` error.

AMIs and their underlying EBS snapshots are physically stored and registered within specific AWS regions. An AMI ID (e.g., `ami-0abcdef1234567890`) only exists within the region where it was created or copied.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can an AMI be copied to a different AWS region, and how?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes, AMIs can be copied cross-region.**

---

### Step-by-Step Procedure:
1. **AWS CLI Command:**
   ```bash
   aws ec2 copy-image      --source-region us-east-1      --source-image-id ami-0a1b2c3d4e5f67890      --region eu-west-1      --name "seller-risk-golden-ami-v1-eu"      --description "Cross-region copy for EU disaster recovery"
   ```
2. **KMS Encryption (Best Practice):**
   - If the source AMI is encrypted with a custom KMS key, you must specify a KMS key ID located in the target region (`--kms-key-id <target-kms-arn>`), because KMS keys are regional.
3. **Result:**
   - AWS copies the underlying EBS snapshot blocks across the AWS backbone to the destination region, registers a new AMI in that region, and assigns a new AMI ID.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: If you can't recall the exact option for copying an AMI to another region, what would you do to make the AMI available in that region?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
If the direct `copy-image` option is not immediately accessible, you can achieve the exact same result through the **underlying EBS snapshot workflow**:

---

### Alternative Method (Snapshot Copy):
1. **Locate the AMI's Root EBS Snapshot:**
   - Inspect the AMI block device mapping to find the backing snapshot ID (`snap-xxxx`).
2. **Copy the Snapshot Cross-Region:**
   ```bash
   aws ec2 copy-snapshot      --source-region us-east-1      --source-snapshot-id snap-0123456789abcdef0      --destination-region us-west-2
   ```
3. **Register a New AMI from the Copied Snapshot in the Target Region:**
   - Once the snapshot finishes copying in `us-west-2`, register the new AMI:
     ```bash
     aws ec2 register-image        --region us-west-2        --name "my-restored-ami"        --root-device-name /dev/xvda        --block-device-mappings DeviceName=/dev/xvda,Ebs={SnapshotId=snap-copied123}
     ```
This works because an AMI is essentially a snapshot plus boot metadata.
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a VPC (Virtual Private Cloud) in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
An **Amazon VPC (Virtual Private Cloud)** is a logically isolated virtual network dedicated to your AWS account. It gives you complete control over your virtual networking environment, resembling a traditional on-premises data center network with the scalability benefits of AWS infrastructure.

---

### Core Components:
1. **IPv4 / IPv6 CIDR Block:** The private IP address range (e.g., `10.0.0.0/16`).
2. **Subnets:** Segments of the VPC IP range bound to specific Availability Zones (Public and Private subnets).
3. **Route Tables:** Routing rules determining where network traffic is directed.
4. **Internet Gateway (IGW):** VPC gateway providing outbound and inbound internet connectivity for public subnets.
5. **NAT Gateway:** Managed gateway enabling instances in private subnets to connect outbound to the internet (for software updates) while preventing external inbound traffic.
6. **Security Groups & NACLs:** Virtual firewalls for instance-level and subnet-level traffic filtering.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you choose the VPC CIDR range and the subnet ranges?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Choosing CIDR ranges requires evaluating **current capacity, future growth, non-overlapping routing with on-premises/other VPCs, and Kubernetes pod density**:

---

### Key Design Guidelines:
1. **RFC 1918 Private Address Spaces:**
   - Choose a large private CIDR, commonly `10.0.0.0/16` (providing 65,536 IP addresses).
2. **Avoid Overlaps with Corporate Networks / Other VPCs:**
   - If planning VPC Peering, AWS Transit Gateway, or AWS Direct Connect/VPN to on-premises data centers, ensure the CIDR block does not collide with existing networks.
3. **Subnet Sizing by Tier & Availability Zones (Spanning 3 AZs):**
   - **Public Subnets (ALBs, NAT Gateways):** Typically smaller (e.g., `/24` = 256 IPs per AZ).
   - **Private Workload Subnets (EKS Nodes & Pods):** Require large address space (e.g., `/20` = 4,096 IPs or `/19` = 8,192 IPs per AZ) due to AWS VPC CNI allocating real VPC IPs to every single container.
   - **Database Subnets (RDS, Redis):** Isolated `/24` or `/26` subnets across 3 AZs.
4. **AWS Reserved IPs:** Remember that AWS reserves **5 IP addresses** in every subnet (Network, Router, DNS, Future use, Broadcast), so a `/28` (16 IPs) leaves only 11 usable IPs.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you actually designed VPC CIDR and subnet ranges before in a real project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes, absolutely.** I designed and provisioned our multi-AZ VPC architecture using Terraform for our production Amazon EKS platform:

---

### Production Design Example:
- **Primary VPC CIDR:** `10.100.0.0/16` (65,536 total IPs).
- **Public Subnets (3 AZs - ALBs & NATs):**
  - AZ-a: `10.100.0.0/24` (251 usable IPs)
  - AZ-b: `10.100.1.0/24` (251 usable IPs)
  - AZ-c: `10.100.2.0/24` (251 usable IPs)
- **Private Subnets (3 AZs - EKS Worker Nodes & Core Infra):**
  - AZ-a: `10.100.16.0/20` (4,091 usable IPs)
  - AZ-b: `10.100.32.0/20` (4,091 usable IPs)
  - AZ-c: `10.100.48.0/20` (4,091 usable IPs)
- **Database Isolated Subnets (3 AZs - RDS PostgreSQL & Redis):**
  - AZ-a: `10.100.64.0/24`
  - AZ-b: `10.100.65.0/24`
  - AZ-c: `10.100.66.0/24`
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: What factors did you consider, and how did you arrive at the number of IPs needed when designing your VPC and subnets?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We factored in four specific mathematical variables:

1. **Number of Microservices & Replicas:** 25 microservices running an average of 3 to 5 replicas during peak traffic (~75-125 application pods).
2. **Kubernetes Cluster DaemonSets & System Pods:** CoreDNS, `kube-proxy`, `aws-node` (VPC CNI), `aws-load-balancer-controller`, `ebs-csi-driver`, `fluent-bit`, `prometheus-node-exporter` (~8-10 pods per node).
3. **Autoscaling Spikes & Rolling Deployments:** During rolling updates (`maxSurge: 25%`), pod counts increase by 25-50%. Karpenter can scale worker nodes from 10 to 40 nodes during high fraud traffic surges.
4. **AWS VPC CNI Prefix Delegation / Secondary IP Pre-allocation:** The AWS VPC CNI plugin maintains a pool of warm secondary private IPs per node ENI (e.g., `WARM_IP_TARGET=5`).
Summing these requirements indicated that a small `/24` subnet would easily risk IP exhaustion, necessitating `/20` subnets.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you decide whether you need a smaller or larger number of IPs for a subnet?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
- **Choose a Smaller Subnet (`/24` to `/27`):**
  - For **Public Ingress and NAT Gateway tiers** where you only host a few Application Load Balancers and NAT gateways.
  - For **Database tiers** (RDS Multi-AZ primary and standby, Redis replication nodes) where instance counts rarely exceed 10-20 instances.
- **Choose a Larger Subnet (`/19` to `/21`):**
  - For **Kubernetes (EKS) private workload subnets** utilizing the AWS VPC CNI, because every container receives its own dedicated IP directly from the subnet.
  - Environments with aggressive autoscaling and ephemeral batch processing workloads.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you give a concrete example from a project you worked on, including the factors you considered and why you chose those specific CIDR ranges?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our **Seller Risk & Abuse Platform**:
- We had **25 microservices** with a minimum of 3 replicas each = 75 pods base.
- During holiday flash sales, HPA scaled high-velocity services (Kafka ingestion and Redis rules) up to 10-15 replicas each, pushing application pod counts to **~250 pods**.
- Add 15 cluster DaemonSets running on ~25 worker nodes = **375 system pod IPs**.
- **The Problem:** In a standard `/24` subnet (251 usable IPs), an EKS cluster spanning 3 AZs would allocate ~80 IPs per AZ. A sudden deployment or node surge in a single AZ would instantly trigger `FailedCreatePodSandBox: no IP addresses available in subnet`.
- **The Solution:** We allocated `/20` subnets (4,091 usable IPs per AZ) for private worker subnets, ensuring 10x headroom for multi-year business growth and zero risk of IP exhaustion.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Which pods and which microservice are you referring to when you say the EKS subnets need multiple IPs for pods?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Under the **AWS VPC CNI** (the default and standard networking plugin for Amazon EKS), **every single pod running in the cluster receives a real private IP address from the VPC subnet**:

- **Application Pods:** Replicas of all 25 microservices:
  - `seller-registration-ingestion-svc` (5 pods)
  - `kafka-order-stream-consumer` (8 pods)
  - `risk-rule-velocity-engine` (12 pods)
  - `ml-anomaly-scoring-svc` (8 pods)
  - `analyst-portal-backend` (4 pods)
  - `enforcement-dispatcher-svc` (3 pods)
- **Cluster System & DaemonSet Pods:**
  - `aws-node` (one per node)
  - `kube-proxy` (one per node)
  - `prometheus-node-exporter` (one per node)
  - `fluent-bit` log forwarder (one per node)
  - `coredns` (4 replicas)
  - `aws-load-balancer-controller` (2 replicas)
Each one consumes an individual private IP from the subnet.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Are you only considering a single service when sizing the IP range for the cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No, absolutely not.** Sizing must account for the **entire aggregate cluster footprint**: all 25 microservices, their autoscaling maximum limits, cluster DaemonSets, operator controllers, and rolling deployment surges across all namespaces.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Do you use a separate Kubernetes cluster for each microservice?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**No.** Running 25 separate Kubernetes clusters for 25 microservices would create immense operational overhead, waste massive cloud compute (paying for 25 EKS control planes and duplicate baseline node pools), and make network communication between services slow and complex.

---

### Our Multi-Tenant Cluster Model:
- We run **shared, multi-tenant EKS clusters** separated by environment (one cluster for Dev/QA, one cluster for Staging, one hardened cluster for Production).
- Microservices are isolated within the cluster using:
  1. **Kubernetes Namespaces:** Logical segregation (e.g., `ingestion`, `detection`, `enforcement`, `monitoring`).
  2. **NetworkPolicies (Calico / VPC CNI):** Enforcing zero-trust network isolation between namespaces.
  3. **ResourceQuotas & LimitRanges:** Preventing any single microservice from consuming all cluster CPU or memory.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How many microservices are deployed in your cluster?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We have **25 microservices** deployed in our production EKS cluster, grouped into the 4 architectural domains:
1. Ingestion & Event Processing (5 services)
2. Risk Rules & Velocity Engine (8 services)
3. Entity Graph & Storage (4 services)
4. Trust & Safety Analyst Portal & Enforcement (8 services)
</details>

#### 【 NETWORKING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: With around 25 microservices running multiple pods, wouldn't 256 IPs be too few for the subnet?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes! Exactly.** A `/24` subnet provides only 256 total addresses, of which only **251 are usable** due to AWS reserving 5 IPs.

With:
- 25 microservices * 3 replicas = 75 application pods.
- 15 worker nodes with 8 DaemonSets each = 120 system pods.
- Worker node Elastic Network Interfaces (ENIs) taking base IPs.
- During rolling updates, pod counts expand by 25-50%.

A `/24` subnet would be at **90%+ IP utilization on Day 1**, leading to catastrophic pod scheduling failures during autoscaling. That is why **we strictly avoid `/24` subnets for EKS worker nodes and instead provision `/20` subnets (4,091 IPs)** or attach a secondary CIDR block.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you decide the CIDR and subnet range when designing the network for a cluster with many services?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We use a deterministic mathematical formula:

$$	ext{Total Subnet IPs Needed} = (	ext{Max Worker Nodes} 	imes 	ext{Max Pods Per Node}) + 	ext{ENI Warm IPs} + (	ext{Active Pods} 	imes 1.5 	ext{ [Surge]}) + 	ext{Buffer}$$

---

### Modern AWS Solution: EKS Custom Networking / Secondary CIDRs:
If an organization already has a VPC constrained by a tight RFC 1918 CIDR (e.g., `/24`), we implement **EKS Custom Networking**:
1. Associate a non-routable secondary CIDR block to the VPC from the Carrier-Grade NAT (CGNAT) space: **`100.64.0.0/16`**.
2. Create large `/19` subnets within this secondary CIDR dedicated purely to Kubernetes pods.
3. Worker nodes stay on the primary private subnets, while all pods receive IPs from the secondary `100.64.0.0/16` space, completely eliminating RFC 1918 IP exhaustion without touching corporate networks.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you done this IP range design in a real project?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes, I have personally designed and implemented this exact architecture in our production AWS environment.** 

I authored the Terraform VPC modules establishing the primary `10.100.0.0/16` network and subsequently implemented the `100.64.0.0/16` secondary CIDR custom networking configuration for our Amazon EKS clusters to support our 25 microservices without IP starvation.
</details>

</details>

</details>

2. **Automated Worker Node Rehydration & OS Patching:**

4. **Self-Healing & Auto-Remediation:**

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you automate the provisioning of Kubernetes infrastructure and maintain Kubernetes clusters in live environments?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We manage the full lifecycle of our Kubernetes clusters through **GitOps, Infrastructure as Code, and automated control plane orchestration**:

---

### 1. Provisioning Automation (Terraform & Terragrunt):
- Clusters are provisioned 100% declaratively using our custom Terraform EKS module:
  - Automates VPC subnets, route tables, IAM roles, OIDC identity provider, and EKS security groups.
  - Installs core add-ons (`vpc-cni`, `kube-proxy`, `coredns`, `aws-ebs-csi-driver`) with automatic version management.
- Parameterized modules enable spinning up an identical staging or disaster recovery cluster in under 20 minutes.

---

### 2. Live Cluster Maintenance & Upgrades:
- **EKS Version Upgrades (e.g., 1.29 to 1.30):**
  - Upgrades are tested first in our staging cluster.
  - Using Terraform, we upgrade the EKS managed control plane in-place (AWS handles high-availability API server rolling updates).
  - Worker nodes are rolled sequentially using Karpenter or managed node group rolling updates with `maxUnavailable: 1`. Pod Disruption Budgets (`PDB`) ensure the required quorum of application pods remains active throughout the upgrade.
- **Node Cordon & Drain Automation:**
  - When performing OS upgrades or instance retirement maintenance, automation invokes:
    ```bash
    kubectl cordon <node-name>
    kubectl drain <node-name> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data --grace-period=60
    ```
  - Workloads smoothly reschedule onto healthy nodes before the retiring node is terminated.
</details>

#### 【 BEHAVIORAL 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you handle incident management in your team?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
We follow a structured, ITIL-aligned Incident Management process designed to restore normal service operations as quickly as possible while maintaining clear communication:

---

### Incident Lifecycle Phases:

#### 1. Identification & Alerting:
- Automated alerts from Prometheus Alertmanager, Datadog, or AWS CloudWatch trigger high-priority alerts via PagerDuty to the on-call engineer.

#### 2. Triage & Severity Classification:
- **Sev-1 (Critical):** Core customer-facing outage (e.g., marketplace checkout down, API 5xx > 5%). SLA: 15-minute response.
- **Sev-2 (Major):** Degradation of non-critical services (e.g., delayed fraud scoring batch, analyst portal slow). SLA: 30-minute response.
- **Sev-3 (Minor):** Internal or non-disruptive bug with a workaround available.

#### 3. Swarming & Communication:
- The Incident Commander opens a dedicated incident Slack channel (`#inc-2026-11-17-api-outage`) and bridges a war room call.
- Stakeholders are updated at 30-minute intervals with a status dashboard update.

#### 4. Mitigation over Root Cause:
- During active incidents, the focus is **rapid mitigation** (e.g., rolling back to the previous stable release, expanding node capacity, or flipping a feature flag) rather than exhaustive forensic debugging.

#### 5. Post-Incident Review (PIR):
- Within 48 hours, a blameless post-mortem is conducted to identify root causes, establish a timeline, and create actionable Jira prevention tickets.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Once you pick up a Jira incident ticket, before you start working on it, how do you report back to management with an estimate of when it will be resolved (e.g., half an hour or three days)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In a professional engineering environment, **you cannot give a definitive resolution time before investigating an unknown incident**, because doing so creates false expectations.

---

### How I Communicate Effectively to Management:
1. **Acknowledge and Set Investigation Windows:**
   - Instead of giving a blind resolution estimate, I provide an **investigation timebox**:
     *"I have picked up the ticket. Based on preliminary alert indicators, I will spend the next 20 to 30 minutes isolating the root cause and will provide an updated ETA and mitigation strategy by 2:30 PM."*
2. **Provide Confidence Ranges based on Incident Classification:**
   - If the issue matches a known pattern (e.g., rollback of a bad deployment): Estimate 15-30 minutes.
   - If the issue requires data patching or deep code fixes: Provide a phased estimate (mitigation within 1 hour; permanent code fix within 1-2 days).
3. **Regular Cadence Updates:**
   - Commit to updating the Jira ticket and incident Slack channel every 30 minutes, keeping leadership informed of what was ruled out and current diagnostic steps.
</details>

#### 【 AI/ML 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you estimate the time needed to resolve an incident before you start investigating it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Prior to deep forensic investigation, estimation relies on **historical pattern matching, impact categorization, and structured heuristics**:

---

### Estimation Heuristics:
1. **Incident Archetype Classification:**
   - **Deployment/Change-Related:** If the incident occurred immediately following a production deployment or config change, the fastest path is an immediate rollback, taking **~15 to 30 minutes**.
   - **Infrastructure/Capacity-Related:** (e.g., disk full, memory exhaustion, EKS node capacity). Remediation via resizing or provisioning takes **~30 to 60 minutes**.
   - **Upstream / Third-Party Outage:** (e.g., AWS regional outage, payment gateway degradation). Resolution depends on vendor SLA; our action is failing over or graceful degradation within **~1 to 2 hours**.
   - **Complex Distributed Bug:** (e.g., silent data corruption, race condition). Requires setting a 45-minute investigation milestone before an accurate resolution estimate can be determined.
2. **Using AI for Historical Ticket Search:**
   - We query internal Jira Knowledge Bases using AI search to match error signatures with past resolved incidents and view their historical Mean Time to Resolution (MTTR).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How does AI produce a plan and investigation for an incident, and what input do you feed to the AI to get it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AI assists incident triage by synthesizing symptoms into a structured diagnostic checklist:

---

### 1. Inputs Fed to the AI (Sanitized):
To protect data privacy and compliance, all proprietary credentials, tokens, customer names, and internal IP ranges are stripped before prompting:
- **Exact Error Message / Stack Trace:** (e.g., `java.sql.SQLTransientConnectionException: HikariPool-1 - Connection is not available, request timed out after 30000ms`).
- **Relevant Kubernetes / AWS Context:** (`EKS 1.30, RDS PostgreSQL db.r6g.xlarge, ALB Ingress 504 Gateway Timeout`).
- **Recent Environmental Changes:** ("Occurred 10 minutes after deploying version 2.4.1 which included new database migration scripts").
- **Metrics Observations:** ("RDS CPU is at 20%, but DatabaseConnections count spiked to the maximum pool limit of 500").

---

### 2. How the AI Produces an Investigation Plan:
The AI processes the inputs against its knowledge base of distributed system failure modes and returns a prioritized investigation plan:
1. **Hypothesis 1:** Connection leak in the newly deployed service code failing to release connections back to the pool.
2. **Diagnostic Actions:** Commands to run (`SELECT count(*), state, query FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY state, query;`).
3. **Immediate Mitigation:** Scale down the new deployment to relieve connection pressure; roll back to previous release.
4. **Verification Step:** Check if ALB 504 errors subside.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you used the AI features of monitoring and observability tools such as Datadog, Splunk, Dynatrace, or New Relic?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes.** Modern enterprise observability platforms have integrated powerful machine learning and AIOps capabilities that we utilize:

1. **Datadog Watchdog / Dynatrace Davis AI:**
   - **Automated Anomaly Detection:** Learns baseline traffic patterns (accounting for seasonal day-of-week and hour-of-day variations) and automatically alerts on anomalous latency spikes or error rates without requiring static hardcoded thresholds.
   - **Root Cause Analysis (RCA):** Correlates sudden application latency with underlying infrastructure metrics (e.g., flagging that a pod latency spike was caused by database lock contention on an RDS instance).
2. **Splunk ITSI / OpenSearch Anomaly Detection:**
   - Uses Random Cut Forest (RCF) machine learning algorithms to detect anomalies in real-time log ingestion streams.
3. **Log Clustering & Noise Reduction:**
   - Automatically clusters millions of disparate log lines into distinct log patterns, instantly highlighting "new or unseen error signatures" that appeared after a deployment.
</details>

#### 【 DOCKER 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you use AI tools in your work with Docker?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I use AI tools to optimize container development, security, and runtime configuration:

1. **Dockerfile Multi-Stage Optimization:**
   - Submitting single-stage, bloated Dockerfiles to an LLM with instructions to convert them into optimized, hardened multi-stage builds.
   - Optimizing layer caching order (e.g., copying `pom.xml` / `package.json` and running dependency downloads *before* copying source code).
2. **Container Security Hardening:**
   - Prompting AI to generate non-root user creation, file permission lockdowns, and minimal distroless/Alpine configurations.
3. **Explaining and Fixing Build Failures:**
   - When complex C-binding or native library compilation errors occur during Docker builds (e.g., Alpine musl vs glibc compatibility errors), AI quickly identifies the missing build dependencies or header packages.
4. **Synthesizing Docker Compose Stacks:**
   - Generating local developer test environments in `docker-compose.yml` linking the application, Redis, Kafka, and PostgreSQL with proper networking and health checks.
</details>

#### 【 MONITORING 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you perform log analysis with the help of AI?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Yes, absolutely.** AI is exceptionally effective at parsing unstructured log data, identifying anomalous patterns, summarizing high-volume stack traces, and suggesting root cause hypotheses:

---

### How AI-Assisted Log Analysis Works:
1. **Log Pattern Clustering:**
   - AI algorithms group 100,000 raw log lines into 5 distinct recurring templates, allowing engineers to focus on the 0.1% novel error patterns.
2. **Contextual Stack Trace Parsing:**
   - Feeding a 50-line nested Java exception stack trace to an LLM, which extracts the root cause (e.g., pinpointing that the root cause was an SSL handshake timeout 4 levels deep in the exception chain).
3. **Log-to-Metric Synthesis:**
   - Helping construct regular expressions and Grok parsing patterns for Fluent Bit or Logstash to extract structured metrics from legacy unstructured log files.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Have you actually performed any AI-assisted log analysis before, or is your answer just a guess?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**I have actually performed AI-assisted log analysis in real production troubleshooting scenarios.**

---

### Concrete Production Example:
We experienced an intermittent issue where one of our Go-based Kafka consumer microservices was silently dropping messages during high traffic bursts with no panic logs in standard application output.

1. **Data Sanitization & Preparation:**
   - I extracted 300 lines of system and container logs from CloudWatch and OpenSearch.
   - Using a local script, I sanitized all sensitive data (PII, seller IDs, IP addresses, Kafka cluster endpoints).
2. **Prompting the LLM:**
   - I provided the sanitized log snippet, the Go Kafka consumer configuration (`confluent-kafka-go`), and the pod's resource limits to the LLM.
   - *Prompt:* "Analyze these container logs and consumer configurations during a traffic spike. Why are messages silently dropping without application panics?"
3. **AI Finding & Root Cause:**
   - The LLM observed subtle TCP socket resets and identified that the Kafka `session.timeout.ms` (30 seconds) was shorter than the batch processing duration during high load spikes.
   - Because the processing thread took 35 seconds to process a batch, Kafka's broker assumed the consumer was dead and initiated a consumer group rebalance, revoking the partition assignment mid-batch.
4. **Resolution:**
   - Tuned `max.poll.interval.ms` to 300,000 ms, increased batch processing concurrency, and completely eliminated the partition revocation drops.

This real-world experience proved how AI drastically shortens the time required to correlate disparate log clues into a verified root cause.
</details>

</details>

</details>

#### 【 SYSTEM DESIGN 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● When should someone choose a Docker/container-based implementation (such as on EKS), and when should they choose an EC2 and EBS based implementation?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The choice between containerized (EKS) and VM-based (EC2 + EBS) architectures depends on workload characteristics, scaling needs, and operational overhead:

### Choose Docker/Containers on EKS when:
1. **Microservices Architecture:** You have multiple decoupled services (e.g., our 25 microservices) that require independent release lifecycles, fast deployments, and separate scaling policies.
2. **High Resource Density & Cost Efficiency:** Multiple small or medium workloads can be bin-packed onto shared EC2 compute nodes, drastically cutting compute waste compared to running dedicated VMs for each service.
3. **Fast Horizontal Scaling:** Microservice pods can scale out in seconds via HPA to handle traffic spikes, with Karpenter dynamically provisioning nodes as needed.
4. **Standardized CI/CD & Portability:** Immutable container images guarantee identical behavior across local development, staging, and production environments.

### Choose EC2 with EBS when:
1. **Legacy or Monolithic Workloads:** Applications that cannot be easily containerized, require specific OS kernel extensions, or have complex systemd-managed daemon dependencies.
2. **High-Performance Stateful Databases:** Self-hosted databases (e.g., large Oracle, Cassandra, or legacy SQL Server) requiring dedicated NVMe disks, sustained ultra-high IOPS (`io2 Block Express`), or specific hardware affinity.
3. **Simple, Low-Complexity Architectures:** A single standalone application or cron worker where the operational complexity of managing Kubernetes control planes, Helm charts, and CNI networking is unjustified.
4. **Specialized Hardware Requirements:** Workloads requiring dedicated GPU passthrough or bare-metal performance without container virtualization layers.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Can you give examples of stateful and stateless applications and explain the difference?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
### Key Differences:
- **Stateless Applications:** Do not maintain client session or persistent data locally. Any pod can handle any incoming request, and pods can be terminated, replaced, or horizontally scaled at any time without data loss.
- **Stateful Applications:** Depend on persistent storage and stable identities (hostname, IP, storage binding) that must survive pod restarts, rescheduling, or node failures.

---

### Concrete Examples from Our Platform:

| Category | Platform Example | Kubernetes Object | Characteristics |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Stateless** | **Event Ingestion Service** | `Deployment` | Consumes marketplace events from Kafka and passes them downstream. Ephemeral; scaled up/down via HPA based on Kafka lag. |
| **Stateless** | **Velocity Rule Engine** | `Deployment` | Evaluates incoming events against dynamic rules in memory; state is stored externally in Redis. |
| **Stateful** | **MongoDB Case Queue** | `StatefulSet` + PVC | Stores analyst review cases. Requires persistent EBS volumes via CSI driver and stable network identities (`mongo-0`, `mongo-1`). |
| **Stateful** | **ElastiCache Redis** / **RDS PostgreSQL** | Managed AWS Services / `StatefulSet` | Maintains in-memory sliding counters and relational seller audit logs requiring ACID durability. |
</details>

#### 【 CLOUD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: From an infrastructure planning perspective (not the application's perspective), how do you determine whether a workload should be hosted on EC2 with EBS versus on EKS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
From an infrastructure engineering standpoint, we evaluate five core criteria:

1. **Resource Utilization & Packing Efficiency:**
   - Hosting 25 microservices on dedicated EC2 instances results in 25 base OS overheads, separate AMIs, and high idle CPU/RAM waste.
   - On EKS, pods are bin-packed onto shared multi-tenant worker nodes, reducing total AWS EC2 infrastructure spend by 40–60%.
2. **Lifecycle & Patching Overhead:**
   - With EC2, platform engineers must build, harden, and rotate AMIs, manage OS security patching, and maintain custom Auto Scaling lifecycle hooks.
   - With EKS, node lifecycle management is abstracted via managed node groups or Karpenter, while applications deploy declaratively without host-level patching dependencies.
3. **Network Topology & IP Allocation:**
   - EC2 uses one primary private IP per instance.
   - EKS (with AWS VPC CNI) assigns secondary private IPs to every pod. Infrastructure planning requires sizing VPC subnets adequately (e.g., adding secondary CIDRs like `100.64.0.0/16`) to prevent IP exhaustion.
4. **Provisioning & Scaling Velocity:**
   - EC2 ASG scale-out requires launching an instance, running user data scripts, and initializing services (2–4 minutes).
   - EKS pod scale-out on pre-warmed nodes takes 5–15 seconds, providing much faster response to unpredictable traffic spikes.
5. **Operational Consistency & Blast Radius:**
   - Containerized workloads on EKS benefit from standardized health checks, automated rolling restarts, and resource quotas, isolating noisy neighbors within the same cluster.
</details>

#### 【 KUBERNETES 】

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How would you justify to your project team that a transaction-heavy banking application should be deployed on EKS instead of EC2?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I would present four key architectural justifications to engineering leadership:

1. **Zero-Downtime Deployments & Rapid Rollbacks:**
   - In banking and financial transactions, service interruptions impact customer trust and violate regulatory SLAs.
   - EKS provides native zero-downtime rolling updates with readiness probes ensuring traffic is only routed to fully initialized pods. If an error rate anomaly is detected, an automated rollback can execute in seconds (`helm rollback`).
2. **Sub-Second Scaling for Flash Transaction Bursts:**
   - Banking platforms experience sudden traffic surges (e.g., salary days, month-end reconciliations, flash sale transactions).
   - HPA scales transaction worker pods in seconds, while Karpenter provisions right-sized nodes in under 45 seconds, preventing transaction queuing and connection drops.
3. **Micro-Segmentation & Regulatory Compliance (PCI-DSS):**
   - Kubernetes NetworkPolicies combined with AWS Security Groups for Pods enforce strict network isolation between payment transaction processing pods and non-sensitive reporting services.
   - Pods run as non-root with read-only root filesystems, significantly reducing the attack surface.
4. **Self-Healing Resiliency:**
   - If a banking processing thread deadlocks or crashes, Kubernetes liveness probes detect the failure and restart the container automatically, eliminating manual operational triage.
5. **Auditability & GitOps Governance:**
   - All infrastructure and deployment configurations are version-controlled in Git, providing a complete, immutable audit trail required by financial compliance regulators.
</details>
