In modern cloud architectures, **SSM Session Manager replaces traditional Bastion Hosts entirely**:
- No public IPs or open inbound ports (port 22 is completely closed).
- No bastion host maintenance or EC2 hourly compute costs.
- Access is authenticated via AWS IAM credentials with multi-factor authentication (MFA) and fully logged in AWS CloudTrail.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How can an application securely access AWS services such as S3?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In AWS, applications should access services like Amazon S3 following the principle of least privilege, zero hardcoded credentials, and private network routing:

---

### 1. Identity & Credential Management: Use IAM Roles (Never Hardcoded Keys)
- **For EC2 / ECS / EKS:** Assign an **IAM Role** to the compute environment:
  - On **EC2:** Attach an IAM Instance Profile.
  - On **Amazon EKS:** Implement **IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA)**, mapping a Kubernetes ServiceAccount to an AWS IAM role via OIDC.
- **Zero Secrets in Code:** The AWS SDK automatically retrieves temporary, rotating security credentials from the AWS Security Token Service (STS) metadata service (`http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`).

---

### 2. Private Network Routing: AWS VPC Gateway Endpoints for S3
- Instead of routing S3 traffic through NAT Gateways and the public internet, provision an **AWS VPC Gateway Endpoint for S3**.
- Traffic travels directly over AWS's private high-speed backbone at **zero cost** and never touches the public internet.

---

### 3. Fine-Grained Least-Privilege IAM & Bucket Policies
- Restrict permissions to the exact bucket and path required:
  ```json
  {
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
      {
        "Effect": "Allow",
        "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject"],
        "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::company-app-data/risk-platform/*"
      }
    ]
  }
  ```

---

### 4. Encryption in Transit and at Rest:
- Enforce TLS (HTTPS) via bucket policy (`aws:SecureTransport: true`).
- Encrypt objects at rest using **AWS KMS Customer Managed Keys (SSE-KMS)** with automated key rotation.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Beyond resource-based bucket policies, what is the next level of security you could apply for an application accessing S3?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Beyond basic S3 bucket policies and IAM roles, enterprise defense-in-depth requires multiple advanced security controls:

---

### 1. VPC Endpoint Policies (Preventing Data Exfiltration):
- A standard VPC Endpoint allows access to *any* S3 bucket in AWS by default. A malicious insider or compromised workload could upload company data to their personal external S3 bucket.
- An **Endpoint Policy** attaches directly to the VPC Gateway Endpoint, strictly restricting which buckets can be accessed through the endpoint:
  ```json
  {
    "Statement": [
      {
        "Sid": "AllowAccessToCorporateBucketsOnly",
        "Effect": "Allow",
        "Principal": "*",
        "Action": "s3:*",
        "Resource": [
          "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-prod-bucket",
          "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-prod-bucket/*"
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
  ```

---

### 2. Condition-Based Bucket Policies (Network-Level Pinning):
- Restrict access to the bucket such that requests are rejected unless they originate from the specific VPC Endpoint ID or specific private VPC:
  ```json
  {
    "Effect": "Deny",
    "Principal": "*",
    "Action": "s3:*",
    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-company-prod-bucket/*",
    "Condition": {
      "StringNotEquals": {
        "aws:sourceVpce": "vpce-0123456789abcdef0"
      }
    }
  }
  ```

---

### 3. S3 Object Lock & Versioning (Ransomware & Tamper Defense):
- Enable **S3 Object Lock** in **Compliance Mode** (WORM - Write Once Read Many), preventing objects from being overwritten or deleted by any user—including the AWS root account—for a mandatory retention period.

---

### 4. AWS S3 Block Public Access (Account & Bucket Level):
- Enable all four S3 Block Public Access settings at the AWS Account level, ensuring no configuration error can ever expose the bucket publicly.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Is there any other way for an application to talk to S3 without going through internet traffic?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes. AWS provides two native private connectivity mechanisms that route all S3 traffic entirely over AWS's internal private network backbone without traversing the internet:

---

### 1. AWS VPC Gateway Endpoint for S3 (Standard & Free)
- **Mechanism:** Created inside the VPC and attached directly to the VPC Route Tables.
- **Routing:** AWS adds a prefix list route (`pl-63a5400a [com.amazonaws.us-east-1.s3]`) pointing to the Gateway Endpoint ID (`vpce-xxxx`).
- **Data Path:** Any EC2 instance, EKS pod, or ECS task in private subnets communicating with `s3.amazonaws.com` is automatically routed over AWS internal optical fibers.
- **Cost:** **100% Free** (No hourly fee, no data transfer charges).

---

### 2. AWS VPC Interface Endpoint for S3 (AWS PrivateLink)
- **Mechanism:** Deploys an Elastic Network Interface (ENI) with a private IP address directly inside your private subnets.
- **Key Use Cases Where Gateway Endpoints Cannot Work:**
  1. **On-Premises Hybrid Connectivity:** On-premises data centers connected to AWS via Direct Connect or Site-to-Site VPN cannot access S3 via Gateway Endpoints. Interface Endpoints allow on-prem servers to talk to S3 using private DNS and private VPC IPs.
  2. **Cross-Region / Cross-VPC via Transit Gateway:** Transit Gateway cannot route traffic to a Gateway Endpoint. Interface Endpoints provide routable private IP endpoints across Transit Gateway attachments.
- **Cost:** Incurs hourly interface endpoint charges ($0.01/hr/AZ) plus per-GB data processing fees.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between an AWS IAM user, group, and role?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) provides three distinct constructs for managing identity and access governance:

---

### Comprehensive Comparison:

| Feature | IAM User | IAM Group | IAM Role |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Definition** | An identity representing an individual human or legacy system requiring long-term access. | A collection of IAM Users used to batch-manage permissions. | An identity with specific permissions that can be **temporarily assumed** by trusted entities. |
| **Credentials** | **Long-term credentials:** Console Password + MFA or permanent Access Key ID & Secret Access Key. | **No credentials.** Groups cannot log in or execute API actions. | **Temporary credentials:** Short-lived tokens generated via AWS Security Token Service (STS) lasting 15 min – 12 hrs. |
| **Used By** | Individual developers, system administrators (though SSO is preferred). | Teams (e.g., `DevOps-Admins`, `Developers`, `Security-Auditors`). | AWS Services (EC2, Lambda, ECS, EKS), Cross-Account access, or Federated Users (Okta, Azure AD). |
| **Can be a Principal?** | Yes (can be granted permissions in resource policies). | **No.** Cannot be referenced as a `Principal` in resource-based policies. | Yes (can be referenced as a `Principal`). |
| **Security Risk** | High if access keys are leaked, committed to Git, or left unrotated. | Medium (inherits member risk). | **Lowest risk:** No long-term stored secrets to leak; credentials expire automatically. |

---

### Production Best Practice:
- **Avoid IAM Users with permanent access keys.** Instead, federate human access via **AWS IAM Identity Center (Single Sign-On)** and grant workloads temporary IAM Roles via Instance Profiles or IRSA.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are IAM roles for ECS tasks in Amazon Elastic Container Service?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Amazon ECS, managing container permissions requires understanding the clear architectural distinction between **Task Execution Roles** and **Task Roles**:

---

### 1. ECS Task Role (`taskRoleArn`):
- **Purpose:** Used by the **application running inside your container** to make authorized AWS API calls.
- **Use Case:** If your containerized Java/Python application needs to read images from an S3 bucket, publish events to SQS, or query a DynamoDB table, those permissions are granted via the Task Role.
- **Security Benefit:** Each container task gets its own fine-grained IAM permissions, avoiding granting broad permissions to the underlying EC2 host instance.

---

### 2. ECS Task Execution Role (`executionRoleArn`):
- **Purpose:** Used by the **Amazon ECS Container Agent** and Docker daemon on the underlying host infrastructure to prepare and launch the task.
- **Permissions Required:**
  - `ecr:GetAuthorizationToken`, `ecr:BatchCheckLayerAvailability`, `ecr:GetDownloadUrlForLayer` (to pull private container images from Amazon ECR).
  - `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` / `ssm:GetParameters` (to fetch database passwords or API keys and inject them as container environment variables).
  - `logs:CreateLogStream`, `logs:PutLogEvents` (to ship container stdout/stderr to Amazon CloudWatch Logs).

---

### Summary Analogy:
The **Task Execution Role** is the *key that lets the landlord (ECS Agent) set up and unlock the apartment*, while the **Task Role** is the *key the tenant (Application) uses to access their private storage unit (S3/DynamoDB)*.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry)?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Amazon Elastic Container Registry (ECR)** is a fully managed, high-performance, and secure Open Container Initiative (OCI)-compliant container image registry provided by AWS.

---

### Key Capabilities & Features:
1. **Tight AWS Ecosystem Integration:**
   - Integrates natively with Amazon EKS, Amazon ECS, and AWS Lambda.
   - Authentication is managed via AWS IAM (`aws ecr get-login-password`), eliminating the need to manage standalone Docker registry passwords.
2. **Automated Vulnerability Scanning:**
   - **Basic Scanning (Clair):** Scans container images for Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) on push.
   - **Enhanced Scanning (Amazon Inspector):** Performs continuous automated package vulnerability scanning and programming language package analysis across all stored images.
3. **Lifecycle Policies (Automated Housekeeping):**
   - Automatically purges untagged images or retains only the last $N$ tagged images (e.g., keep the last 30 builds), preventing runaway storage costs.
4. **Encryption & High Availability:**
   - Encrypts images at rest using AWS KMS (Customer Managed Keys).
   - Replicates images automatically across Availability Zones and provides Cross-Region / Cross-Account replication for disaster recovery.
5. **Private and Public Repositories:**
   - Supports private corporate repositories with VPC Endpoint access and public image hosting on AWS Public Gallery.
</details>

#### 【 LINUX 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the different run levels in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In traditional Linux SysVinit systems, a **Runlevel** represents a predefined operating state of the operating system defining which system services and daemons are active.

---

### Traditional SysVinit Runlevels (0–6):

| Runlevel | Name / Target Mode | Description |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **0** | **Halt** | Shuts down and powers off the system completely. |
| **1 / S** | **Single-User Mode** | Maintenance mode with root access only; no networking, no daemons. Used for filesystem repair or root password recovery. |
| **2** | **Multi-User (No Network)** | Multi-user text console mode without network services (rarely used). |
| **3** | **Multi-User with Networking** | **Standard headless production server mode:** Full multi-user CLI with complete networking enabled. |
| **4** | **Undefined / Custom** | Reserved for custom user-defined operational configurations. |
| **5** | **Graphical Mode (GUI)** | Multi-user mode with full networking and X11/Wayland graphical desktop environment. |
| **6** | **Reboot** | Shuts down and reboots the operating system. |

---

### Modern Systemd Equivalents (Systemd Targets):
Modern Linux distributions (RHEL 7+, Ubuntu 16.04+, Amazon Linux 2023) replace SysVinit runlevels with **Systemd Targets**:
- Runlevel 0 ➔ `poweroff.target`
- Runlevel 1 ➔ `rescue.target`
- Runlevel 3 ➔ `multi-user.target`
- Runlevel 5 ➔ `graphical.target`
- Runlevel 6 ➔ `reboot.target`

---

### Essential Management Commands:
```bash
# Check current runlevel
who -r   # or: runlevel

# Check default systemd target
systemctl get-default

# Switch to multi-user CLI mode dynamically
sudo systemctl isolate multi-user.target

# Set default boot target to headless CLI server
sudo systemctl set-default multi-user.target
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you check disk usage in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Linux system administration, disk usage is analyzed at two levels: **Filesystem-level capacity** and **Directory/File-level consumption**:

---

### 1. Filesystem-Level Disk Usage (`df`):
```bash
# Check disk space across all mounted filesystems in human-readable format (GB, MB)
df -h

# Check inode utilization (crucial when disk has space but throws "No space left on device")
df -ih

# Display filesystem type (ext4, xfs, overlay) alongside mount points
df -hT
```

---

### 2. Directory & File-Level Space Consumption (`du`):
```bash
# Display summary size of a specific directory
sudo du -sh /var/log

# Identify the top 10 largest subdirectories within /var
sudo du -h --max-depth=1 /var 2>/dev/null | sort -hr | head -n 10

# Stay on current filesystem and avoid mounted network shares / virtual filesystems
sudo du -xh -d 1 / | sort -hr | head -n 10
```

---

### 3. Finding Large Individual Files (`find`):
```bash
# Find all files larger than 500MB across the filesystem
sudo find / -type f -size +500M -exec ls -lh {} + 2>/dev/null | awk '{print $9, $5}'
```

---

### 4. Interactive Terminal Tool:
- Using **`ncdu`** (NCurses Disk Usage) for fast interactive visual drill-down:
  ```bash
  sudo apt-get install -y ncdu && ncdu /
  ```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you view running processes in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Viewing and managing running processes in Linux is accomplished using both static snapshot tools and real-time interactive monitors:

---

### 1. Static Process Inspection (`ps`):
```bash
# Standard BSD-style format: show all processes with user, PID, CPU%, MEM%, and command
ps aux

# Standard System V format: show full process hierarchy with Parent Process ID (PPID)
ps -ef

# Sort top 10 processes consuming the most CPU
ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -n 11

# Sort top 10 processes consuming the most Memory
ps aux --sort=-%mem | head -n 11

# Filter for a specific process (e.g., java or nginx)
ps aux | grep [j]ava
```
*(Tip: Using `[j]ava` prevents grep itself from appearing in the output).*

---

### 2. Fast Process Search (`pgrep` & `pidof`):
```bash
# Find the Process ID of all running Nginx instances
pgrep nginx

# List process ID and process name
pgrep -l -u root
```

---

### 3. Process Hierarchy & Trees (`pstree`):
```bash
# Visualize process parent-child relationships in an ASCII tree
pstree -p
```

---

### 4. Real-Time Interactive Monitors:
- **`top`**: Native real-time task manager displaying system uptime, load averages, CPU/memory stats, and tasks. Press `M` to sort by memory, `P` to sort by CPU.
- **`htop`**: Modern, enhanced interactive visual process viewer with colored metrics, mouse support, and tree views.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you change file permissions in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
File permissions in Linux define read (`r`), write (`w`), and execute (`x`) privileges for the **User (owner)**, **Group**, and **Others**. Permissions are modified using the **`chmod`** command.

---

### 1. Numeric (Octal) Mode:
Permissions are represented by octal digits calculated by summing values:
- `r` (Read) = **4**
- `w` (Write) = **2**
- `x` (Execute) = **1**
- `-` (No permission) = **0**

```bash
# 755: Owner (rwx=7), Group (r-x=5), Others (r-x=5) - Standard for executables/directories
chmod 755 deploy.sh

# 644: Owner (rw-=6), Group (r--=4), Others (r--=4) - Standard for regular files
chmod 644 config.yaml

# 600: Owner (rw-=6), Group (none=0), Others (none=0) - Standard for private SSH keys
chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_rsa

# 700: Owner (rwx=7), Group (none=0), Others (none=0) - Standard for .ssh directories
chmod 700 ~/.ssh
```

---

### 2. Symbolic Mode:
Uses references (`u` = user, `g` = group, `o` = others, `a` = all) with operators (`+` add, `-` remove, `=` set):
```bash
# Add execute permission to the owner
chmod u+x script.sh

# Remove write permission from group and others
chmod go-w application.log

# Grant read and execute to everyone
chmod a+rx binary
```

---

### 3. Recursive Permission Changes:
```bash
# Recursively apply permissions to all files and subdirectories
chmod -R 755 /var/www/html
```

---

### 4. Changing Ownership (`chown`):
Permissions often accompany ownership changes:
```bash
# Change owner and group
sudo chown -R appuser:appgroup /opt/myapp
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How would you find which process is using a particular port in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
To identify which process is listening on or bound to a specific network port (e.g., port `8080`), you can use any of the following production tools:

---

### 1. Using `lsof` (List Open Files - Fastest & Cleanest):
```bash
sudo lsof -i :8080 -P -n
```
- `-i :8080`: Filters for network connections on port 8080.
- `-P`: Inhibits conversion of port numbers to port names (e.g., prints 80 instead of http).
- `-n`: Inhibits DNS hostname lookups, making execution instantaneous.

**Sample Output:**
```text
COMMAND   PID    USER   FD   TYPE DEVICE SIZE/OFF NODE NAME
java    14290 appuser   45u  IPv6 429103      0t0  TCP *:8080 (LISTEN)
```

---

### 2. Using `ss` (Socket Statistics - Modern Replacement for netstat):
```bash
sudo ss -tulpn | grep :8080
```
- `-t`: TCP sockets.
- `-u`: UDP sockets.
- `-l`: Listening sockets only.
- `-p`: Shows the process name and PID using the socket.
- `-n`: Numeric ports and IPs.

---

### 3. Using `netstat`:
```bash
sudo netstat -tulpn | grep :8080
```

---

### 4. Using `fuser` (Identify & Kill Fast):
```bash
# Check PID on port
sudo fuser 8080/tcp

# Kill the process holding the port directly
sudo fuser -k 8080/tcp
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you list cron jobs in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Cron jobs in Linux exist at both the individual user level and the system-wide level:

---

### 1. Listing User-Specific Cron Jobs:
```bash
# List cron jobs for the currently logged-in user
crontab -l

# List cron jobs for a specific user (requires root/sudo privileges)
sudo crontab -u deployer -l
sudo crontab -u root -l
```

---

### 2. Inspecting System-Wide Cron Directories:
System-level and package-installed cron jobs are stored across predefined configuration files:
```bash
# Inspect the main system crontab
cat /etc/crontab

# List modular application crontabs
ls -la /etc/cron.d/
cat /etc/cron.d/*

# Inspect hourly, daily, weekly, and monthly automated scripts
ls -la /etc/cron.hourly/
ls -la /etc/cron.daily/
ls -la /etc/cron.weekly/
ls -la /etc/cron.monthly/
```

---

### 3. Modern Systemd Timers (Replacing Cron):
On modern systemd Linux distributions, many scheduled tasks run as **systemd timers**:
```bash
# List all active systemd timers with next scheduled trigger time
systemctl list-timers
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between a hard link and a soft link in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Linux, links create references to files stored in the filesystem. The distinction between Hard Links and Soft Links (Symbolic Links) centers on **Inodes (Index Nodes)**:

---

### Comprehensive Comparison:

| Feature | Hard Link | Soft Link (Symbolic Link / Symlink) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Inode Association** | Points directly to the **exact same inode number** as the target file. | Has its **own distinct, independent inode** containing a text path to the target. |
| **Creation Command** | `ln target.txt hardlink.txt` | `ln -s target.txt symlink.txt` |
| **Original File Deletion** | **Data remains completely intact.** The file data is only erased when the hard link count drops to 0. | **Becomes broken / dangling link.** Points to a non-existent path. |
| **Cross-Filesystem** | **Cannot cross filesystems or partitions.** (Inodes are only unique within a single filesystem). | **Can span across different filesystems, partitions, and network mounts.** |
| **Directory Linking** | Cannot link directories (preventing infinite recursive filesystem loops). | **Can freely link directories.** |
| **File Size** | Displays the identical file size as the original file. | Displays a tiny file size (equal to the number of characters in the target path string). |

---

### Practical Verification Example:
```bash
$ touch file.txt
$ ln file.txt hard.txt
$ ln -s file.txt soft.txt
$ ls -li
# Output:
# 102431 -rw-r--r-- 2 user user 0 Nov 20 15:00 file.txt  <-- Inode 102431 (Link count 2)
# 102431 -rw-r--r-- 2 user user 0 Nov 20 15:00 hard.txt  <-- Same Inode 102431
# 102489 lrwxrwxrwx 1 user user 8 Nov 20 15:01 soft.txt -> file.txt <-- New Inode 102489
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you view mounted file systems in Linux?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
There are several standard ways to view mounted filesystems, storage devices, and mount options in Linux:

---

### 1. Using `findmnt` (Most Modern & Human-Readable):
```bash
# Display mounted filesystems in a hierarchical tree view
findmnt

# Filter by filesystem type (e.g., ext4, xfs)
findmnt -t ext4,xfs
```

---

### 2. Using `df` (Disk Free with Filesystem Types):
```bash
# Displays mounted filesystems with disk usage, size, and filesystem type
df -hT
```

---

### 3. Using `lsblk` (Block Devices & Mount Points):
```bash
# Lists all block devices, partitions, UUIDs, filesystem formats, and mount targets
lsblk -f
```

---

### 4. Using the `mount` Command:
```bash
# Lists all active mounts formatted cleanly in columns
mount | column -t

# Filter for real physical disks (excluding virtual sysfs, proc, devtmpfs)
mount | grep -E "^/dev"
```

---

### 5. Inspecting Kernel Mount Tables:
```bash
# Read live kernel mount table
cat /proc/mounts
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is LVM (Logical Volume Manager) in Linux and what is it used for?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**Logical Volume Manager (LVM)** is a storage management and virtualization framework for Linux that provides a dynamic, flexible abstraction layer between raw physical disks and the filesystem.

---

### 1. The Three-Tier LVM Architecture:
```text
[ Physical Disks / Partitions ] :  /dev/sdb1      /dev/sdc1
               │                         │             │
               ▼                         ▼             ▼
[ Physical Volumes (PV) ]       :     [ PV 1 ]      [ PV 2 ]
                                         └──────┬──────┘
                                                ▼
[ Volume Group (VG) ]           :      [ App_Volume_Group ] (Pooled Capacity)
                                         ┌──────┴──────┐
                                         ▼             ▼
[ Logical Volumes (LV) ]        :   [ web_lv ]    [ db_lv ]
                                         │             │
                                         ▼             ▼
[ Filesystems & Mounts ]        :    /var/www      /var/lib/mysql
```

1. **Physical Volume (PV):** Raw block devices, hard drives, or partitions initialized for LVM (`pvcreate /dev/sdb1`).
2. **Volume Group (VG):** The storage pool created by aggregating one or more Physical Volumes together (`vgcreate data_vg /dev/sdb1 /dev/sdc1`).
3. **Logical Volume (LV):** Virtual partitions carved out from the Volume Group's storage pool, formatted with a filesystem (ext4/XFS), and mounted to directories (`lvcreate -L 100G -n app_lv data_vg`).

---

### 2. What is LVM Used For?
- **Online Dynamic Volume Expansion:** Resize partitions on a running production server without taking services offline or unmounting disks:
  ```bash
  sudo lvextend -r -L +50G /dev/data_vg/app_lv
  ```
  *(The `-r` flag resizes the underlying ext4/XFS filesystem automatically in one step).*
- **Storage Pooling across Disks:** Combine disks of different physical sizes into a single unified storage pool.
- **LVM Snapshots:** Create instantaneous read-only or read-write point-in-time snapshots for zero-downtime database backups.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How good are you at shell scripting?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
I consider myself **highly proficient (8.5 out of 10)** in Bash and shell scripting, using it daily for cloud operations, container entrypoints, and CI/CD automation.

---

### Production Scripting Standards I Enforce:
1. **Defensive Error Handling:** Always initializing scripts with:
   ```bash
   set -euo pipefail
   IFS=$'
	'
   ```
   - `-e`: Exit immediately if any command returns a non-zero exit status.
   - `-u`: Treat unset variables as errors and exit immediately.
   - `-o pipefail`: Catch failures occurring inside pipelines (e.g., `cmd1 | cmd2`).
2. **Signal Trapping & Graceful Cleanup:**
   Using `trap` to ensure temporary files, background subshells, and lock files are cleaned up on `EXIT`, `SIGINT`, or `SIGTERM`:
   ```bash
   trap 'rm -rf "${TMP_DIR}"; echo "[INFO] Cleanup completed."' EXIT
   ```
3. **Modular Functions & Logging:** Writing reusable, parameterized functions with standardized timestamps and log levels (`[INFO]`, `[WARN]`, `[ERROR]`).
4. **Command-Line Argument Parsing:** Using `getopts` or `while-case` loops to handle CLI flags, options, and help usage menus cleanly.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you run a shell script?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Linux, running a shell script depends on execution permissions and whether the script should execute in a new subshell or the current shell environment:

---

### 1. Direct Execution via Shebang (Recommended Production Standard):
1. Grant execute permissions:
   ```bash
   chmod +x deploy.sh
   ```
2. Execute specifying the path:
   ```bash
   ./deploy.sh          # Relative path
   /opt/bin/deploy.sh   # Absolute path
   ```
- The kernel inspects the first line (**shebang**, e.g., `#!/usr/bin/env bash`), launches that interpreter, and runs the script in a new child subshell.

---

### 2. Invoking via Interpreter (Does Not Require Execute Permissions):
```bash
bash deploy.sh
sh deploy.sh
```
- Launches a new bash subshell and executes the script even if `deploy.sh` does not have `chmod +x` permissions.

---

### 3. Sourcing in the Current Shell (`source` or `.`):
```bash
source ./env_vars.sh
. ./env_vars.sh
```
- **Crucial Distinction:** Sourcing **does NOT spawn a child subshell**. It executes commands directly within the **current shell environment**, meaning environment variables and aliases defined inside the script persist in your active terminal session.

---

### 4. Running as a Background or Detached Process:
```bash
# Run in background
./long_job.sh &

# Run immune to hangups (terminal disconnect) with logging
nohup ./long_job.sh > output.log 2>&1 &
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are the special variables in shell scripting that use the dollar sign, such as $$, $0, $#, $*, $@ and $?, and what are they used for?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Bash shell scripting, special parameters prefixed with `$` provide contextual metadata about the script's execution, arguments, and process state:

---

### Special Variables Reference Table:

| Variable | Name | Description & Use Case |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **`$?`** | **Exit Status** | Stores the exit status of the **most recently executed command**. `0` indicates success; any value `1–255` indicates an error. Used extensively in `if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then` checks. |
| **`$$`** | **Process ID (PID)** | Stores the **PID of the currently executing shell script**. Frequently used to create unique temporary filenames, e.g., `TMP_FILE="/tmp/data_$$.tmp"`. |
| **`$0`** | **Script Name** | The name of the script as it was invoked from the command line. Commonly used in usage/help functions: `echo "Usage: $0 {start|stop}"`. |
| **`$#`** | **Argument Count** | The **total number of positional arguments** passed to the script. Used to validate input arguments: `if [ "$#" -lt 2 ]; then`. |
| **`$@`** | **All Arguments (Individual)** | Expands all positional arguments as **separate words** (`"$1" "$2" "$3"`). **Best practice for iterating over arguments** in `for` loops because it preserves argument spaces. |
| **`$*`** | **All Arguments (Single String)** | Expands all positional arguments as a **single merged string** separated by the first character of `IFS` (`"$1 $2 $3"`). |
| **`$!`** | **Last Background PID** | The Process ID (PID) of the most recently executed background job. Used to wait for background tasks: `wait $!`. |
| **`$_`** | **Last Argument** | Expands to the last argument of the previous command executed. |

---

### Practical Example Demonstrating `$@` vs `$*`:
```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Run: ./test.sh "apple pie" "banana split"

echo "Using \$* (Combined):"
for item in "$*"; do
    echo " -> $item"   # Prints: -> apple pie banana split (1 iteration)
done

echo "Using \$@ (Preserves quoting - Correct):"
for item in "$@"; do
    echo " -> $item"   # Prints: -> apple pie
                       #         -> banana split (2 iterations)
done
```
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is a Terraform state file and why is it important?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **Terraform State File** (`terraform.tfstate`) is a critical JSON document maintained by Terraform that serves as the single source of truth mapping your declared HCL configuration code to real-world cloud resources.

---

### Why is the State File Important?

1. **Mapping Configuration to Real-World Resources:**
   - Terraform code declares abstract resource blocks (e.g., `resource "aws_vpc" "main"`). The state file stores the actual physical cloud identifiers assigned by AWS (e.g., `vpc-0a1b2c3d4e5f67890`), resource attributes, and current metadata.
2. **Tracking Metadata & Dependencies:**
   - It maintains a complete dependency graph between resources. When you modify or destroy resources, Terraform inspects the state file to calculate the exact order in which actions must occur without breaking dependencies.
3. **Performance Optimization & Caching:**
   - For infrastructures with hundreds of resources, querying the AWS API for every single resource on every plan operation would trigger aggressive API rate throttling. The state file acts as a local cache of attributes.
4. **Drift Detection:**
   - During `terraform plan`, Terraform compares the declared code against the state file and the live cloud API to accurately identify what was added, changed, or removed out-of-band.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Describe the lifecycle of a Terraform resource, including the Terraform workflow commands.</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The lifecycle of an infrastructure resource managed by Terraform moves through well-defined phases guided by the core Terraform workflow commands:

---

### The Core Terraform Workflow Commands:

1. **`terraform init` (Initialization Phase):**
   - Initializes the working directory, downloads required provider plugins (e.g., `hashicorp/aws`), initializes the configured backend (S3/DynamoDB), and downloads declared child modules.
2. **`terraform validate` (Verification Phase):**
   - Validates that the configuration files are syntactically valid and internally consistent without accessing remote APIs.
3. **`terraform plan` (Speculative Execution Phase):**
   - Refreshes state against live infrastructure, determines the delta between current state and desired configuration, and generates an execution plan listing actions: `+` (create), `~` (update in-place), `-` (destroy), or `-+/+-` (replace).
4. **`terraform apply` (Provisioning Phase):**
   - Executes the planned changes against provider APIs, creates/updates the real cloud resources, and atomically writes the updated attributes back into the state file.
5. **`terraform destroy` (Decommissioning Phase):**
   - Deletes all real-world resources tracked in the state file in reverse topological dependency order and purges the state.

---

### Resource Lifecycle States:
- **Declared:** Resource is written in `.tf` files.
- **Planned:** Terraform creates an execution graph.
- **Creating ➔ Created:** API call succeeds; resource is recorded in state.
- **Drifted:** Resource modified out-of-band in AWS Console.
- **Updated / In-Place Modified:** Non-destructive changes applied.
- **Replaced (`create_before_destroy` or destroy-then-create):** Changes to immutable attributes (e.g., EC2 AMI) require tearing down and recreating.
- **Destroyed:** Terminated in AWS and removed from state.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What are Terraform backends and how do they help in state management?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
A **Terraform Backend** defines where and how Terraform stores, retrieves, and locks its state file.

By default, Terraform uses the **local backend**, storing `terraform.tfstate` on the local machine's disk—which is dangerous and unsuitable for team environments.

---

### Production Standard: Remote Backends (e.g., AWS S3 + DynamoDB)
```hcl
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "company-tfstate-us-east-1-prod"
    key            = "platform/eks/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-lock-table"
    encrypt        = true
  }
}
```

---

### How Remote Backends Help in State Management:

1. **Centralized Single Source of Truth:**
   - Prevents team members and CI/CD pipelines from operating on stale or out-of-sync local state files.
2. **Distributed State Locking:**
   - Integrates with DynamoDB to acquire an exclusive lock during write operations (`plan` / `apply`), preventing simultaneous executions from corrupting the state file.
3. **Data Security & Secret Protection:**
   - Enforces encryption at rest using AWS KMS Customer Managed Keys and encryption in transit via TLS.
   - Access is tightly governed via AWS IAM policies, keeping sensitive database passwords or certificates out of local developer machines.
4. **Automated State Versioning & Disaster Recovery:**
   - With S3 Object Versioning enabled, every state modification creates a historical version, allowing instant rollback in the event of accidental state corruption.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with Terraform modules?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I work with **Terraform Modules** on a daily basis to enforce DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself) principles, establish standard architectural patterns, and maintain consistent infrastructure across development, staging, and production environments.

---

### 1. Custom Internal Enterprise Modules:
I author and maintain modular building blocks for our platform:
- **`modules/vpc`:** Encapsulates VPC creation, public/private subnets, NAT Gateways, and route tables.
- **`modules/eks`:** Provisions EKS control plane, node groups, KMS cluster encryption, and OIDC provider.
- **`modules/rds_postgres`:** Configures Multi-AZ RDS, parameter groups, DB subnet groups, and automated snapshot retention.

---

### 2. Standard Module Directory Structure:
```text
modules/aws_vpc/
├── main.tf         # Resource definitions
├── variables.tf    # Strictly typed inputs with descriptions & validations
├── outputs.tf      # Exported attributes (e.g., vpc_id, private_subnet_ids)
├── versions.tf     # Required Terraform and provider constraints
└── README.md       # Usage documentation and input/output tables
```

---

### 3. Open-Source Registry Modules:
I also leverage verified community modules from the **Terraform Registry** (such as `terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws` and `terraform-aws-modules/eks/aws`) for foundational cloud primitives, calling them with company-specific configuration parameters.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: How do you handle dependencies between Terraform modules, for example when a VPC module must be created before an EC2 module?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In Terraform, managing inter-module dependencies is best handled using **Output-to-Input Chaining (Implicit Dependency)** rather than explicit overrides:

---

### 1. Implicit Dependencies via Outputs & Inputs (Best Practice):
Terraform automatically builds a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) by analyzing resource references. When module B references an output from module A, Terraform automatically provisions module A first:

```hcl
# 1. VPC Module exports private subnet IDs
module "vpc" {
  source   = "./modules/vpc"
  vpc_cidr = "10.0.0.0/16"
}

# In modules/vpc/outputs.tf:
# output "private_subnet_ids" {
#   value = aws_subnet.private[*].id
# }

# 2. EC2 Module accepts the private subnet ID as an input parameter
module "ec2_cluster" {
  source         = "./modules/ec2_instance"
  instance_count = 3
  subnet_id      = module.vpc.private_subnet_ids[0] # <-- Dependency created here!
  security_group = module.vpc.internal_sg_id
}
```
Because `module.ec2_cluster` consumes `module.vpc.private_subnet_ids[0]`, Terraform guarantees that the VPC and its subnets are completely created before provisioning the EC2 instances.

---

### 2. Explicit Dependencies (`depends_on`):
In rare scenarios where no direct attribute is shared (e.g., an IAM Role module must finish before an S3 bucket policy references it), use the `depends_on` meta-argument:
```hcl
module "app" {
  source     = "./modules/app"
  depends_on = [module.iam_roles]
}
```

---

### 3. Decoupled State Architecture (Cross-Layer State):
When VPC and Compute reside in separate, decoupled Terraform state files, pass outputs between them using **`terraform_remote_state`** data sources or **AWS SSM Parameter Store**:
```hcl
data "terraform_remote_state" "vpc" {
  backend = "s3"
  config = {
    bucket = "company-tfstate-prod"
    key    = "network/vpc/terraform.tfstate"
    region = "us-east-1"
  }
}

# Reference in Compute configuration
subnet_id = data.terraform_remote_state.vpc.outputs.private_subnet_ids[0]
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you used Terraform's force-unlock command, and what is it used for?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have used the **`terraform force-unlock`** command in operational incident response.

---

### What is `force-unlock` and When is it Used?
- When Terraform begins a `plan` or `apply` operation using a remote backend with locking (DynamoDB), it acquires an exclusive lock identified by a unique **Lock ID** (UUID).
- Under normal conditions, Terraform releases the lock upon completion.
- However, if the process is abruptly killed—for example, if a Jenkins CI runner runs out of memory (OOMKilled), a network partition disconnects the runner, or an engineer terminates the terminal session mid-execution—the lock remains stuck in DynamoDB.
- Subsequent runs will fail with:
  ```text
  Error: Error acquiring the state lock: ConditionalCheckFailedException
  Lock Info:
    ID: 9b1e4c7a-8f2d-4e9b-b67a-123456789abc
  ```

---

### How to Safely Force-Unlock:
```bash
terraform force-unlock 9b1e4c7a-8f2d-4e9b-b67a-123456789abc
```

---

### Production Safety Precautions Before Running:
1. **Verify No Process is Actually Running:** Confirm with team members and check CI/CD pipeline dashboards to guarantee that no build runner or developer is genuinely running an `apply`.
2. **Never Force-Unlock While an Apply is in Progress:** Forcibly removing the lock while an apply is writing to state will lead to corrupted state and duplicate cloud resources.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between the terraform destroy and terraform refresh commands?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Both commands interact with the state file and live cloud resources, but they have opposite objectives:

---

### Comprehensive Comparison:

| Feature | `terraform destroy` | `terraform refresh` |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Primary Objective** | **Decommission & delete infrastructure:** Destroys all managed cloud resources. | **Synchronize state:** Reconciles the state file with the actual current state in AWS. |
| **Impact on Real Cloud Resources** | **Destructive:** Makes API delete calls to terminate EC2, drop RDS tables, delete subnets. | **Non-destructive:** Makes read-only API calls (`describe`, `get`) to inspect resources. |
| **State File Modification** | Removes destroyed resources from the state file (leaving it empty or cleaned). | Updates state attributes (e.g., updated IPs, tags, statuses) without altering declared code. |
| **Command Status** | Active core command: `terraform destroy -auto-approve`. | **Deprecated as standalone command** since Terraform v0.15; replaced by `terraform plan -refresh-only`. |

---

### Modern Best Practice for Refreshing:
Avoid running standalone refresh because it immediately overwrites state without review. Instead, use:
```bash
# Safely inspect drift before applying state changes
terraform plan -refresh-only

# Apply the refreshed state safely
terraform apply -refresh-only
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Why is the terraform import command used?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The **`terraform import`** command is used to bring existing real-world cloud infrastructure—provisioned manually via the AWS Management Console, CLI, or legacy CloudFormation templates—under **Terraform state management** without having to destroy and recreate the resources.

---

### How it Works:
1. Write the resource block in your HCL code:
   ```hcl
   resource "aws_s3_bucket" "audit_logs" {
     bucket = "my-company-audit-logs"
   }
   ```
2. Execute the import command specifying the Terraform address and physical AWS ID:
   ```bash
   terraform import aws_s3_bucket.audit_logs my-company-audit-logs
   ```
3. Terraform queries the AWS S3 API, retrieves the bucket's current metadata, and writes the resource entry directly into `terraform.tfstate`.

---

### Modern Alternative: Terraform 1.5+ Declarative `import {}` Blocks
In modern Terraform versions, imports can be defined declaratively in code and committed to Git:
```hcl
import {
  to = aws_s3_bucket.audit_logs
  id = "my-company-audit-logs"
}
```
Running `terraform plan -generate-config-out=generated.tf` will even automatically generate the HCL configuration code!
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Why do we need to import resources into Terraform if they already exist in AWS?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Even though the resource exists and functions perfectly in AWS, **Terraform is completely blind to it until it is recorded in the state file**:

---

### 1. Terraform Relies on State as its Single Source of Truth:
- Terraform does not scan your entire AWS account to figure out what you own; it strictly inspects its **state file**.
- If you write `resource "aws_vpc" "existing"` in your code without importing it, Terraform assumes it does not exist and attempts to create a brand new VPC upon `apply`, resulting in naming collisions or duplicate resources.

---

### 2. Preventing Accidental Overwrites & Deletion:
- Once imported into state, Terraform can track drift, enforce security baselines, and prevent manual out-of-band changes from compromising the system.

---

### 3. Adopting Infrastructure as Code (IaC) without Production Downtime:
- In enterprise migrations, critical production resources (e.g., an enterprise RDS PostgreSQL database or core VPC) cannot be deleted and recreated just to get them into Terraform. Importing provides a **zero-downtime adoption path**.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with Terraform workspaces to manage multiple environments, and how do they work?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have worked with **Terraform Workspaces**, understanding both how they function and their operational limitations in enterprise production:

---

### 1. How Terraform Workspaces Work:
- Workspaces allow a single Terraform configuration directory to manage multiple distinct state files.
- By default, Terraform uses the `default` workspace.
- Managing workspaces via CLI:
  ```bash
  terraform workspace new dev
  terraform workspace new prod
  terraform workspace select dev
  ```
- **Backend Storage:** In remote backends (S3), workspace state files are isolated under a dedicated prefix:
  `s3://my-bucket/env:/<workspace_name>/terraform.tfstate`.
- **HCL Interpolation:** You can dynamically adjust variables based on the active workspace:
  ```hcl
  instance_type = terraform.workspace == "prod" ? "m5.large" : "t3.micro"
  ```

---

### 2. Limitations in Production & The Recommended Alternative:
- **Why Workspaces are Risky for Multiple Environments:**
  - All environments share the exact same backend credentials and identical configuration code, increasing the blast radius. A developer intending to apply changes to `dev` could easily run `terraform apply` against `prod` if they forget to switch workspaces.
  - Leads to unreadable code filled with ternary conditionals.
- **Enterprise Best Practice (Directory-Based Separation / Terragrunt):**
  - Instead of workspaces, separate environments by directory:
    ```text
    environments/
    ├── dev/     # Isolated S3 backend, dev.tfvars, Dev IAM credentials
    ├── staging/ # Isolated S3 backend, staging.tfvars
    └── prod/    # Isolated S3 backend, prod.tfvars, strict Prod IAM lock
    ```
  - This provides true physical isolation, separate state files, and zero risk of cross-environment human error.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you handle resource failures and retries in Terraform when resource creation or update fails?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Resource failures during a `terraform apply` can occur due to cloud API throttling (HTTP 429), eventual consistency delays, missing dependencies, or invalid configuration parameters.

---

### Comprehensive Remediation Strategy:

1. **Leverage Terraform's Idempotent Execution:**
   - Terraform commits state incrementally. If resource 1 through 5 succeed and resource 6 fails, the first 5 are **already safely stored in the state file**.
   - Simply fixing the root cause and re-running `terraform apply` will resume from the point of failure without recreating the first 5 resources.
2. **Resolving Cloud Eventual Consistency Delays:**
   - Certain AWS resources (e.g., IAM role attachments, route table associations) take several seconds to propagate across AWS control planes. If a dependent resource fails immediately:
     - Add an explicit **`depends_on`** block to force Terraform to wait for the prerequisite resource.
     - Or introduce a `time_sleep` resource from the `hashicorp/time` provider:
       ```hcl
       resource "time_sleep" "wait_30_seconds" {
         depends_on      = [aws_iam_role_policy_attachment.eks_node]
         create_duration = "30s"
       }
       ```
3. **Configuring Custom Resource Timeouts:**
   - For long-running operations (e.g., EKS node group provisioning or RDS cluster creation), extend timeout thresholds:
     ```hcl
     timeouts {
       create = "45m"
       delete = "30m"
     }
     ```
4. **Targeted Applies for Quick Unblocking (`-target`):**
   - For isolated debugging in lower environments:
     ```bash
     terraform apply -target=aws_security_group.app_sg
     ```
   *(Used only for emergency triage, not standard CI/CD).*
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the difference between local Terraform modules and remote modules?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
The difference between Local and Remote Terraform Modules centers on **source location, versioning independence, and organizational reusability**:

---

### Comprehensive Comparison:

| Feature | Local Module | Remote Module |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Source Definition** | Uses a relative filesystem directory path: `source = "./modules/vpc"`. | Uses a remote Git repository, Terraform Registry, or S3 bucket URL: `source = "github.com/org/terraform-aws-vpc?ref=v2.1.0"`. |
| **Download Behavior** | Copied directly from the local filesystem during `terraform init`. | Cloned and downloaded into the local `.terraform/modules` directory during `terraform init`. |
| **Versioning & Immutability** | Tied directly to the parent repository commit; cannot be version-pinned independently. | **Independently versioned using Git release tags** (`?ref=v1.4.0`), ensuring immutable, breaking-change-safe releases. |
| **Organizational Sharing** | Usable only within the same repository. | **Shared across hundreds of repositories** and multiple engineering teams across the organization. |
| **Development Speed** | Fast feedback; local edits are tested immediately without pushing to Git. | Requires committing, pushing, and tagging in a remote repository before consuming updates. |

---

### Production Standard:
Use **Remote Modules** with semantic version pinning (`?ref=v1.2.0`) in enterprise CI/CD pipelines to prevent unexpected upstream changes from breaking production deployments.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the purpose of Terraform's lifecycle block and when would you use it?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Terraform's **`lifecycle`** block allows engineers to override default resource behavior and control how Terraform manages resource creation, updates, and destruction.

---

### Core Lifecycle Meta-Arguments & Production Use Cases:

#### 1. `create_before_destroy = true`
- **Default Behavior:** Terraform destroys the existing resource first, then creates the replacement (causing downtime).
- **With `create_before_destroy`:** Terraform provisions the new replacement resource first, verifies it is healthy, and only then destroys the old resource.
- **Use Case:** Zero-downtime updates for EC2 Launch Templates, Auto Scaling Groups, and AWS ACM SSL/TLS certificates.

#### 2. `prevent_destroy = true`
- **Default Behavior:** Terraform will destroy any resource if removed from configuration.
- **With `prevent_destroy`:** Terraform throws an immediate hard error if any `plan` or `apply` would cause the resource to be deleted.
- **Use Case:** Critical production safeguards on stateful resources (e.g., Production RDS PostgreSQL databases, S3 compliance audit buckets).

#### 3. `ignore_changes = [...]`
- **Default Behavior:** Terraform detects any out-of-band attribute difference and reverts it to match HCL code.
- **With `ignore_changes`:** Instructs Terraform to ignore specific attribute drifts.
- **Use Case:**
  - Auto Scaling Groups where `desired_capacity` is dynamically scaled by Karpenter or AWS Auto Scaling policies.
  - AWS resources where external security scanners or Datadog inject tags out-of-band:
    ```hcl
    lifecycle {
      ignore_changes = [tags["LastScanned"], desired_capacity]
    }
    ```

#### 4. `replace_triggered_by = [...]`
- Forces resource replacement when another resource or attribute changes (e.g., recreate an EC2 instance whenever an external user-data template file hash changes).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Which Terraform version are you currently using?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our current production platform, we are running **Terraform v1.6.x** (with active testing on **v1.7+ / v1.9** for forward compatibility).

---

### Key Modern Terraform Features We Actively Use:
1. **Declarative `import {}` Blocks (Terraform 1.5+):** 
   - Moving away from imperative CLI commands (`terraform import`) to version-controlled import definitions checked into Git.
2. **`removed {}` Blocks (Terraform 1.7+):**
   - Allows safely removing resources from Terraform state management without deleting the underlying physical cloud resources in AWS.
3. **`check {}` Blocks (Continuous Validation):**
   - Writing assertion blocks that continuously validate infrastructure health (e.g., verifying that an ALB endpoint returns HTTP 200) without failing the plan.
4. **Enhanced Provider Input Validation:**
   - Using native `validation {}` blocks with custom error messages inside input variable definitions.
</details>

#### 【 CI/CD 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● How have you integrated Terraform into your CI/CD pipeline?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In our organization, all infrastructure changes are automated through a **Jenkins Declarative Pipeline** integrated with Git Pull Requests, eliminating manual CLI executions:

---

### Two-Stage CI/CD Architecture for Terraform:

```text
[ Developer PR Raised ]
         │
         ▼
[ Jenkins PR (CI) Pipeline ]
  ├── 1. terraform fmt -check (Style enforcement)
  ├── 2. tflint (Syntax & deprecated provider warnings)
  ├── 3. tfsec / Checkov (Static security scanning)
  ├── 4. terraform init -backend=false
  ├── 5. terraform plan (Generates speculative plan)
  └── 6. Post plan output as a comment to GitHub PR
         │
[ 2-Admin PR Approval & Merge ]
         │
         ▼
[ Jenkins Release (CD) Pipeline ]
  ├── 1. terraform init (Initializes remote S3 backend)
  ├── 2. terraform plan -out=tfplan (Generates immutable binary plan)
  ├── 3. [Manual Approval Gate] (Interactive review in Jenkins UI for Prod)
  ├── 4. terraform apply tfplan (Applies exact planned binary)
  └── 5. Send Slack notification on deployment status
```

---

### Pipeline Security & Best Practices Implemented:
1. **Plan Output as PR Comment:** Developers and reviewers inspect the exact resource additions, updates, and deletions directly on GitHub before approving.
2. **Immutable Plan File (`-out=tfplan`):** Applying the pre-generated binary plan file guarantees that the changes reviewed during the plan phase are the **exact changes applied**, preventing race conditions.
3. **Temporary IAM Credentials:** Jenkins build agents assume dedicated AWS IAM roles via AWS STS with short-lived tokens; no long-lived AWS keys reside on runners.
</details>

#### 【 IAC 】

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you used the Terraform taint and untaint commands, and what are they used for?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have used both `terraform taint` and its modern replacement in operational workflows:

---

### 1. What Does `terraform taint` Do?
- The `taint` command manually marks a specific managed resource inside the state file as degraded or damaged.
- On the next `terraform apply`, Terraform **forces the destruction and recreation** of that tainted resource, even if its configuration in `.tf` files has not changed at all.
- Command:
  ```bash
  terraform taint aws_instance.worker_node
  ```

---

### 2. What Does `terraform untaint` Do?
- If a resource was accidentally tainted, running `untaint` removes the tainted marker from the state file, preventing its recreation:
  ```bash
  terraform untaint aws_instance.worker_node
  ```

---

### 3. Modern Best Practice: `terraform apply -replace`
Starting in **Terraform v0.15.2+**, HashiCorp introduced the **`-replace`** flag, making `taint` largely legacy:
```bash
terraform plan -replace="aws_instance.worker_node"
terraform apply -replace="aws_instance.worker_node"
```
- **Why `-replace` is Better:** It does not mutate the state file prior to running the plan. If you cancel the plan, no state change occurred, whereas `taint` immediately mutates the state file.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● What is the use of Terraform .tfvars files for variable assignment?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
**`.tfvars` (Terraform Variable Definitions) files** are plain-text files used to supply concrete values to input variables declared in `variables.tf`.

---

### 1. Why Use `.tfvars` Files?
- **Decoupling Logic from Values:** Keeps infrastructure code generic and reusable. The same `.tf` configurations can provision Development, Staging, and Production environments simply by passing different `.tfvars` files.
- **Environment Isolation:**
  - `dev.tfvars`: `environment = "dev"`, `instance_type = "t3.micro"`, `min_nodes = 2`.
  - `prod.tfvars`: `environment = "prod"`, `instance_type = "m5.large"`, `min_nodes = 10`.

---

### 2. Loading `.tfvars` Files:
- **Automatically Loaded:**
  - Any file named `terraform.tfvars`.
  - Any file matching `*.auto.tfvars` (e.g., `common.auto.tfvars`).
- **Explicitly Loaded via CLI Flag (CI/CD Standard):**
  ```bash
  terraform apply -var-file="environments/prod.tfvars"
  ```

---

### 3. Variable Precedence Order in Terraform:
When variables are defined across multiple sources, Terraform resolves them in the following order (highest precedence wins):
1. **`-var` and `-var-file` CLI flags** (Highest)
2. `*.auto.tfvars` (alphabetical order)
3. `terraform.tfvars.json`
4. `terraform.tfvars`
5. `TF_VAR_<name>` Environment Variables
6. Default values in `variables.tf` (Lowest)
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How do you implement automated testing for Terraform code?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Automated testing for Terraform infrastructure code follows a **DevSecOps testing pyramid** spanning static checks, security compliance, unit testing, and full integration tests:

---

### The Infrastructure Testing Pyramid:

1. **Static Analysis & Formatting (Pre-Commit / PR):**
   - `terraform fmt -check`: Enforces canonical indentation and HCL formatting.
   - `terraform validate`: Verifies syntax and internal variable consistency.
   - **TFLint:** Linters that catch provider-specific errors (e.g., invalid EC2 instance types, missing required tags).

2. **Security & Policy as Code (SAST):**
   - **Tfsec / Checkov / Trivy:** Scans HCL code for security misconfigurations (e.g., S3 buckets without encryption, open security groups `0.0.0.0/0:22`, unencrypted EBS volumes).
   - **Open Policy Agent (OPA) / Conftest:** Enforces organizational compliance guardrails before infrastructure is provisioned.

3. **Native Unit & Mock Testing (Terraform 1.6+):**
   - Uses the native **`terraform test`** framework with `.tftest.hcl` files.
   - Supports testing variable validations, resource attribute calculations, and outputs using **provider mocks** without making real AWS API calls.

4. **Integration & End-to-End Testing (Terratest):**
   - Using **Terratest** (Go-based testing library developed by Gruntwork):
     1. Spawns real, ephemeral AWS infrastructure in an isolated test account (`terraform.InitAndApply`).
     2. Validates real-world behavior (e.g., makes an HTTP GET request to the created ALB and asserts HTTP 200).
     3. Automatically destroys the infrastructure in a `defer` block (`terraform.Destroy`).
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you used data blocks (data sources) in Terraform, and what are they used for?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, **Data Blocks (`data "..."`)** are read-only queries in Terraform used to fetch and consume information about resources that exist **outside of the current Terraform configuration**.

---

### Key Production Use Cases:

1. **Querying Dynamic AMI IDs (Always Use Latest Hardened Image):**
   ```hcl
   data "aws_ami" "amazon_linux_2023" {
     most_recent = true
     owners      = ["amazon"]
     filter {
       name   = "name"
       values = ["al2023-ami-2023.*-x86_64"]
     }
   }
   ```

2. **Querying Existing Network Components (VPC, Subnets):**
   ```hcl
   data "aws_vpc" "shared_vpc" {
     tags = {
       Environment = "production"
     }
   }
   ```

3. **Fetching Caller Identity & AWS Account Metadata:**
   ```hcl
   data "aws_caller_identity" "current" {}
   data "aws_region" "current" {}

   # Usage in IAM policies:
   # "arn:aws:iam::${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}:root"
   ```

4. **Reading Outputs from Another Terraform State (`terraform_remote_state`):**
   - Allows decoupled microservice states to safely read shared VPC subnet IDs or database connection strings.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you used Terraform's destroy timeout attribute to control resource destruction?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have configured custom **resource timeouts** using the `timeouts` block to control the maximum duration Terraform will wait when creating, updating, or destroying cloud resources.

---

### 1. Why is the Destroy Timeout Necessary?
- By default, Terraform enforces built-in default timeouts (typically 20 minutes).
- Certain stateful or complex AWS resources take significantly longer to decommission:
  - **Amazon RDS Aurora Clusters:** Database teardown, automated final snapshot creation, and KMS key de-registration can exceed 30 minutes.
  - **AWS CloudFront Distributions:** Must be disabled first and wait for worldwide edge cache propagation before deletion, frequently exceeding default limits.
  - **Amazon EKS Managed Node Groups:** Pod draining, node cordoning, and ALB target group deregistration can hang if pods have stubborn termination grace periods.
- Without custom destroy timeouts, Terraform times out with an error, leaving the infrastructure in a half-destroyed, locked state.

---

### 2. Configuration Example:
```hcl
resource "aws_db_instance" "production_db" {
  identifier        = "prod-postgres-db"
  allocated_storage = 100
  engine            = "postgres"
  instance_class    = "db.r6g.xlarge"

  timeouts {
    create = "60m"
    update = "40m"
    delete = "2h" # Extends deletion timeout to 2 hours
  }
}
```
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Have you worked with Terragrunt?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Yes, I have hands-on experience with **Terragrunt**, an open-source, thin orchestrator and wrapper for Terraform created by Gruntwork that provides tooling to keep configurations DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself), manage multi-environment state, and orchestrate inter-module dependencies.

---

### Key Advantages & Features of Terragrunt:

1. **DRY Remote State Backend Configuration:**
   - In pure Terraform, every environment directory must repeat the `backend "s3" {}` block.
   - In Terragrunt, you define the remote state once in a root `terragrunt.hcl`. Child directories inherit it automatically via `include`:
     ```hcl
     # root terragrunt.hcl
     remote_state {
       backend = "s3"
       config = {
         bucket         = "company-tfstate-${get_aws_account_id()}"
         key            = "${path_relative_to_include()}/terraform.tfstate"
         region         = "us-east-1"
         dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"
       }
     }
     ```

2. **DRY Module Calls (Promoting Immutable Code):**
   - Instead of duplicating resource blocks across `dev/` and `prod/`, Terragrunt points to a single versioned Git repository and supplies environment-specific `inputs`:
     ```hcl
     terraform {
       source = "git::git@github.com:company/infrastructure-modules.git//eks?ref=v2.4.0"
     }
     inputs = {
       cluster_name = "prod-eks-cluster"
       node_count   = 10
     }
     ```

3. **Inter-Module Dependency Orchestration:**
   - Using the `dependency` block, Terragrunt automatically fetches outputs from prerequisite modules and applies changes across multiple stacks in topological order using **`terragrunt run-all apply`**:
     ```hcl
     dependency "vpc" {
       config_path = "../vpc"
     }
     inputs = {
       vpc_id     = dependency.vpc.outputs.vpc_id
       subnet_ids = dependency.vpc.outputs.private_subnet_ids
     }
     ```
</details>

</details>

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Are you currently working rotational shifts, and what is your current shift arrangement?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
In my current role supporting the Intuit Marketplace Risk Platform:

---

### 1. Current Shift Schedule:
- **Standard Schedule:** My primary schedule is a **general day shift (typically 09:30 AM to 06:30 PM IST)** aligned with our core software engineering squads and daily Agile ceremonies.
- **Rotational On-Call Support:** Because our E-Commerce Risk Detection Platform operates 24/7, our platform team maintains a **weekly rotational on-call schedule** (via PagerDuty and Slack) to handle high-severity production alerts (P1/P2) outside standard hours.
- **Scheduled Maintenance Windows:** For critical infrastructure changes (e.g., EKS control plane upgrades, major RDS PostgreSQL maintenance, or networking cutovers), we rotate coverage for late-evening or weekend maintenance windows.

### 2. Flexibility & Shift Adaptability:
- Having worked across global enterprise environments, I am **fully adaptable and willing to work in rotational shifts, 24/7 on-call schedules, or US-overlapping shift models** as required to maintain continuous production uptime.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● Can you tell me more about your previous two jobs, apart from your current role?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Prior to my current assignment at Intuit (via Genox Technologies), I accumulated over 5 years of progressive experience across two key professional phases:

---

### 1. Simvoysys — DevOps & Cloud Engineer:
- **Core Focus:** Cloud infrastructure automation, containerization, and CI/CD pipelines on AWS.
- **Key Contributions:**
  - **AWS Infrastructure Provisioning:** Designed and managed multi-AZ AWS environments using modular Terraform (VPCs, private subnets, EC2 Auto Scaling, RDS PostgreSQL Multi-AZ, S3, IAM policies, and Application Load Balancers).
  - **Containerization & Kubernetes:** Built optimized multi-stage Dockerfiles for microservices, deployed and managed containerized workloads on Kubernetes clusters, and configured Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA) and ingress controllers.
  - **CI/CD Pipeline Engineering:** Implemented declarative Jenkins pipelines integrating Git, Maven, SonarQube quality analysis, Trivy vulnerability scanning, and automated container image publishing to Amazon ECR.
  - **Observability:** Deployed Prometheus and Grafana dashboards to monitor cluster health, node CPU/memory saturation, and HTTP error rates.

### 2. Initial Foundational Role — Systems & Linux Administrator / Junior DevOps Engineer:
- **Core Focus:** Enterprise Linux server administration, infrastructure maintenance, networking, and shell scripting.
- **Key Contributions:**
  - **Linux Server Administration:** Managed RHEL/CentOS and Ubuntu servers, including user access control (RBAC), LVM disk partitioning, SSH hardening, systemd service management, and cron job scheduling.
  - **Automation Scripting:** Authored modular Bash shell scripts for automated server maintenance, log rotation, system health monitoring, and database backup routines.
  - **Networking & Security:** Configured foundational network protocols (DNS, DHCP, TCP/IP, firewalls/iptables) and managed SSL/TLS certificate installations.
  - **Developer Deployment Support:** Assisted development teams with manual and semi-automated code deployments, environment configuration, and Git repository branching management.

---

### Key Takeaway:
This progressive journey provided me with a strong bottom-up foundation—starting with the Linux kernel, networking, and system internals, advancing through cloud automation and containerization, up to enterprise-scale Kubernetes platform engineering on EKS.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>● How many people are there in the team you work in?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Our organizational structure consists of a dedicated platform pod supporting a wider cross-functional engineering group:

- **Immediate DevOps & Platform Team:** We are a focused team of **5 engineers** (including myself, senior platform engineers, and a platform lead).
- **Supported Engineering Organization:** Our platform pod directly supports the broader **Marketplace Seller Risk & Abuse Detection Platform**, which comprises approximately **30 to 35 engineers** across backend microservice squads, frontend teams, QA automation engineers, data engineers, and risk analysts.
- **Operating Model:** We operate as centralized platform enablers—building shared CI/CD libraries, maintaining Terraform modules, managing Kubernetes clusters, and resolving infrastructure escalations so development squads can deploy safely and independently.
</details>

<details>
<summary><strong>↳ Follow-up: Besides DevOps, what other roles exist in your team or the wider team you work with?</strong></summary>

**Answer:**
Within our broader cross-functional product and platform ecosystem, I collaborate daily across several specialized technical disciplines:

---

### Key Collaborating Roles:

1. **Backend Software Engineers (Java & Go):**
   - Build, maintain, and optimize the 22–25 core microservices (e.g., Event Ingestion Service, Velocity Rule Engine, Anomaly Scoring Service, Case Queue Service, Enforcement Orchestration Service).
   - Collaborate with DevOps on Dockerfile optimizations, health check endpoints (`/healthz`, `/readyz`), and resource request/limit sizing.

2. **Frontend Engineers (React.js):**
   - Develop the internal Trust & Safety analyst web portals where risk analysts review flagged accounts, inspect entity relationship graphs, and execute manual enforcement actions.

3. **QA & Automation Test Engineers:**
   - Author end-to-end API, functional, and performance test suites (Locust, Postman, Selenium) integrated directly into our Jenkins CI pre-merge pipelines.

4. **Data Engineers & Data Scientists:**
   - Develop real-time fraud detection models, maintain Kafka stream processing pipelines, and configure feature stores for risk anomaly scoring.

5. **Trust & Safety / Risk Analysts (Business Operations):**
   - Domain experts who review prioritized case queues, identify emerging fraud trends, and provide feedback on false-positive rates to fine-tune velocity rules.

6. **Product Managers & Scrum Masters:**
   - Define business roadmaps, prioritize sprint backlogs in Jira, lead daily standups, and coordinate release milestones across teams.
